\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}(\hbar^{\infty})
as claimed.

Finally, to estimate
\|p_{\eta}\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}\times L^{\infty}_{x}}=\mathcal{O}(\eta)
, we observe that by assumption,
\partial_{x,\xi}\mathcal{H}\neq 0
for
x\in\{\mathcal{H}=0\}
, so that
\big|\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\mathcal{H}(x,\xi)|\leq\mathrm{C}\delta\big|=\mathcal{O}(\delta)
for any
0<\delta\leq\underline{\mathrm{c}}
for some small constant
\underline{\mathrm{c}}
.
Then, by considering dyadic scales
\delta_{k}=\eta 2^{k}
for
k\geq 0
, we deduce from the estimate (
3.16
) that uniformly for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\int|p_{\eta}(x,\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi=\mathcal{O}\bigg(\eta+\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{\eta^{2}\delta_{k}}\bigg)=\mathcal{O}(\eta)

since
\hbar^{2}\eta^{-4}\leq 1
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

From Proposition
3.2
, by applying the stationary phase
for mild symbols (in the form of
Proposition
A.6
), one can prove the
relevant commutator estimates when the spectral scale
\eta
is larger
than
\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
, in which case we only need to assume that

\varepsilon\eta\geq\hbar
. We focus on (
3.2
).

Since
f_{\eta}(H_{\hbar})
and
g_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{w})
are
self-adjoint, from the expression of the kernel of
f_{\eta}(H_{\hbar})
in Proposition

3.2
, we obtain

\|[f_{\eta}(H_{\hbar}),g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w})]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}\\
=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)}{\hbar}}p_{\eta}(x,\xi)p_{\eta}(y,\zeta)\chi(x-y)^{2}(g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(x))-g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(y)))^{2}\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty}).

(3.18)

We split this integral in two parts by introducing a cutoff. Let
\chi_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth compactly supported function, let
\chi_{\varepsilon}:=\chi(\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{w})
and
\chi^{\dagger}_{\varepsilon}:=1-\chi_{\varepsilon}
be such that
\chi^{\dagger}_{\varepsilon}\cdot g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w})=0
.
We have

\|[f_{\eta}(H_{\hbar}),g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w})]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int p_{\eta}(x,\xi)\bigg(\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)}{\hbar}}p_{\eta}(y,\zeta)Q(x,y)\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}y\bigg)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi\\
+\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int p_{\eta}(y,\zeta)g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(y))^{2}R(y,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty})

(3.19)

where

\displaystyle Q:(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\chi(x-y)^{2}\chi_{\varepsilon}(x)(g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(x))-g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(y)))^{2}

\displaystyle R:(y,\zeta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)}{\hbar}}\chi(x-y)^{2}p_{\eta}(x,\xi)\chi_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi.

The relevant property is that, with
\ell=2
,

(y,\zeta)\mapsto\varepsilon^{-1}p_{\eta}(y,\zeta)\int Q(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\text{ is in $S^{\varepsilon}_{y}\times S^{\eta}_{\zeta}$ with $\varepsilon\eta\geq\hbar$ and $\partial_{y}^{k}Q(x,y)|_{y=x}=0$ for $k<\ell$}.

(3.20)

Then, applying Corollary
A.7
with
\ell=2
, we obtain (uniformly for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
)

\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)}{\hbar}}p_{\eta}(y,\zeta)Q(x,y)\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}\big(\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{\eta^{2}\varepsilon}\big).

Since

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int|p_{\eta}(x,\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi=\mathcal{O}(\eta),

this implies that

\eqref{J2comm1}=\mathcal{O}\big(\tfrac{\hbar^{2-n}}{\eta\varepsilon}\big)
.

Then, the amplitude of the integral
R
is in
S^{\delta}_{x}\times S^{\eta}_{\xi}
with
\delta=\min\{\varepsilon,\eta\}
, so applying either Proposition
A.6
in case
\delta=\varepsilon
or Lemma
A.2
in case
\delta=\eta
with
\eta\geq\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
(here,
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}(x)=0
for
x\in B(y,\varepsilon)
if
y\in\{g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w})\neq 0\}
),we obtain in both cases

R(y,\zeta)=\mathcal{O}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\eta\varepsilon}\big)^{\infty}\big)

with the required uniformity.
Thus, this implies that the second line of (
3.19
) is
\mathcal{O}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\eta\varepsilon}\big)^{\infty}\hbar^{-n}\eta\varepsilon\big)
. Combining these estimates, this concludes the proof of (
3.2
).

Now, we turn to the estimate (
3.3
); the argument
being the same. Using Proposition
3.2
, we also have

\|[[f_{\eta}(H_{\hbar}),g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w})],k_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w})]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int p_{\eta}(x,\xi)\bigg(\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)}{\hbar}}p_{\eta}(y,\zeta)\widetilde{Q}(x,y)\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}y\bigg)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi\\
+\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int p_{\eta}(y,\zeta)g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(y))^{2}k_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(y))^{2}\widetilde{R}(y,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty})

(3.21)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}:(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\chi(x-y)^{2}\chi_{\varepsilon}(x)(g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(x))-g_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(y)))^{2}(k_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(x))-k_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{w}(y)))^{2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}:(y,\zeta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)}{\hbar}}\chi(x-y)^{2}p_{\eta}(x,\xi)\chi_{\varepsilon}^{\dagger}(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi.

Now
\widetilde{Q}
satisfies the condition (
3.20
) with
\ell=4
.
Thus, the first term of the right-hand-side of (
3.21
)
is
\mathcal{O}\big(\tfrac{\hbar^{4-n}}{\eta^{3}\varepsilon^{3}}\big)
and the integral involving
\widetilde{R}
is again
\mathcal{O}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\eta\varepsilon}\big)^{\infty}\hbar^{-n}\eta\varepsilon\big)
.
This yields the estimate (
3.3
).

4
Hilbert-Schimdt norm of commutators: Proof of Theorem
1
.

Throughout this section, we use the notation from Section
2.1
.
In particular,
\Omega\Subset\mathcal{D}
is a fixed relatively
compact open subset of the bulk with smooth boundary,

f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and we denote
f|_{\Omega}=f\mathds{1}_{\Omega}
(this function is also viewed as a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
).
Using Proposition
2.5
, we focus on analysing the commutator
[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]
where the regularized kernel
\widetilde{\Pi}
is given by the oscillatory integral (
2.3
).
Then, with the phase (
3.5
), one has

\displaystyle\big\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\tr\big([\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}][f|_{\Omega},\widetilde{\Pi}^{*}]\big)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n+2}}\int e^{i\frac{\Psi_{0}(x,y,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},t_{1},t_{2},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}{\hbar}}A_{0}(x,y,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},t_{1},t_{2},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

(4.1)

\displaystyle(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathds{1}_{\lambda_{1}\leq\mu}\mathds{1}_{\lambda_{2}\leq\mu}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

where the amplitude
A_{0}
belongs to
S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{6n+4})
and its principal part at
(t_{1},t_{2},y)=(0,0,x)
is given by

A_{0}(x,x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},0,0,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})|_{\hbar=0}=\vartheta(V(x)+|\xi_{1}|^{2})\vartheta(V(x)+|\xi_{2}|^{2})\chi(\lambda_{1})\chi(\lambda_{2}).

(4.2)

Moreover, since
\Omega\Subset\{V<0\}
, the function
(x,y)\mapsto(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathds{1}\{|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell}\}
is supported in
\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime}
where
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\{V<-2\underline{\mathrm{c}}\}
is a neighbourhood of
\Omega
in the bulk. Consequently, by (
2.1
),
A_{0}
is supported in

\big\{t\in[-\underline{\tau},\underline{\tau}],(x,y)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime},|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell},|\xi_{1}|,|\xi_{2}|\geq\underline{\mathrm{c}},|\lambda_{1}|,|\lambda_{2}|\leq\underline{\ell}\}.

(4.3)

for small constants
\underline{\tau}\ll\underline{\ell}\ll\underline{\mathrm{c}}
.

The proof consists of the following steps.

•

In Section
4.1
, we gather some preliminary estimates for singular integrals with discontinuous amplitudes.

•

In Section
4.2
, using the stationary phase method, we perform a series of reductions to write the oscillatory integral (
4.1
) as

\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\frac{-1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n}).

(4.4)

where the function
F
has compact support, is smooth on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{\xi=0\}
and
|F(x,y,\xi)|\leq C|\xi|
.

•

In Section
4.3
, we study integrals of the form
(
4.4
) in the particular case where the set
\Omega
is
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphic to
the ball
B=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|x|<1\}
.
After a change of variables and further reduction steps, we show that

\eqref{red}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}g(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathds{1}_{B}(x)\mathds{1}_{B}(y)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n}),

where
g
is smooth on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}\setminus\{\xi=0\}
and
g(v,\xi)=|\xi|R(v)+\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{2})
as
\xi\to 0
.
Using the asymptotics of the Fourier transform
\widehat{\mathds{1}}_{B}
, which is related to Bessel functions, we obtain that the leading term of (
4.4
) is given by

(2\pi\hbar)^{1-n}\frac{\log\hbar^{-1}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\partial B}R(\hat{x})f(\hat{x})^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{x}.

•

Finally, in Section
4.4
, we use a partition of unity type argument to conclude the proof of
Theorem
1
without the topological assumption.

•

In Section
4.5
, we prove Theorem
2
. The Gaussian asymptotic fluctuations for counting statistics is a direct consequence of Theorem
1
(the variance of
\mathbf{X}(\Omega)
diverges for any smooth set
\Omega
) and we only need to estimate covariances.
Using the off-diagonal decay of the regularized kernel
(Proposition
4.17
), we argue that for two smooth sets

\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}\Subset\mathcal{D}
such that
|\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2}|=0
, the covariance satisfies
\tr\big([\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}][\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}},\Pi]\big)=o(\log(\hbar^{-1})\hbar^{1-n})
as
\hbar\to 0
.

4.1
Preliminary estimates

In this section we gather several estimates that will be important for
the proof of Theorem
2
.

Lemma 4.1
.

There exists a constant
C_{n}
such that the following is true: let

\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded open set with a smooth
boundary. Denote by
|\partial\Omega|
the
(n-1)
-Hausdorff measure of
the smooth, compact hypersurface
\partial\Omega
. Then for every

\hbar>0
sufficiently small,

\int_{|x-y|\leq\hbar}\frac{|\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}}{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int_{|x-y|\leq\hbar}\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\sim C_{n}|\partial\Omega|\hbar^{n+1}.

For any
\varkappa:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to[0,1]
with compact support,

\int\frac{|\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}}{2|x-y|^{n}}\varkappa(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int\frac{\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)}{|x-y|^{n}}\varkappa(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

(4.5)

Moreover, given
\overline{\chi}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
smooth such that
\overline{\chi}(x)=0
for
|x|\leq\underline{\mathrm{c}}
, there exists a constant
C
so that for any
\hbar>0
,

\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})\frac{|\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}}{2|x-y|^{n+2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})\frac{\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)}{|x-y|^{n+2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq C/\hbar.

(4.6)

Proof.

Let

\Omega_{\hbar}=\{x\in\Omega:\dist(x,\partial\Omega)<\hbar\}

be an
\hbar
-neighborhood of
\partial\Omega
inside
\Omega
.
Since
\partial\Omega
is smooth and compact, if
\hbar
is small enough, then the following change of variables is well defined

\displaystyle\Omega_{\hbar}

\displaystyle\mapsto\partial\Omega\times(0,1)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(\hat{x},t)=\big(\underset{q\in\partial\Omega}{\arg\min}|q-x|^{2},\hbar^{-1}\dist(x,\partial\Omega)\big).

This corresponds to the (orthogonal) projection of
x\in\Omega_{\hbar}
onto

\partial\Omega
and the volume form
\mathrm{d}x\sim\hbar\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\hat{x}
as
\hbar\to 0
where
\mathrm{d}\hat{x}
denotes the volume measure on
\partial\Omega
.
Hence, as
\hbar\to 0

\int\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{|x-y|\leq\hbar}\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\sim\hbar\int_{\partial\Omega\times(0,1)}\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathds{1}_{|\hat{x}-\hbar t\nu_{\Omega}(\hat{x})-y|\leq\hbar}\mathrm{d}y\bigg)\mathrm{d}\hat{x}\mathrm{d}t

where
\nu_{\Omega}(\hat{x})
denotes the (exterior) unit normal vector at
\hat{x}\in\Omega
.

For a fixed
\hat{x}\in\partial\Omega
and
t>0
,

\int_{\Omega^{c}}\mathds{1}_{|\hat{x}-\hbar t\nu_{\Omega}-y|\leq\hbar}\mathrm{d}y\sim\hbar^{n}\int_{\{y\cdot\mathrm{e}_{1}\geq 0\}}\mathds{1}_{|y+t\mathrm{e}_{1}|\leq 1}\mathrm{d}y

which follows by rescaling and using normal coordinates around
\hat{x}
.
This shows that there exists a constant
C_{n}
so that as
\hbar\to 0
,

\int_{|x-y|\leq\hbar}\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\sim C_{n}\hbar^{n+1}\int_{\partial\Omega}\mathrm{d}\hat{x}.

To obtain (
4.5
), observe that for
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

\int\frac{\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)}{|x-y|^{s}}\varkappa(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}:2^{k}\leq C/\hbar}(\hbar 2^{k})^{-s}\int_{|x-y|\leq\hbar 2^{k+1}}\hskip-8.5359pt\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

where the constant
C
depends only on
\varkappa
.
The pervious argument shows that the integrals on the RHS are
\mathcal{O}\big((\hbar 2^{k})^{n+1}\big)

so that

\int\frac{\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)}{|x-y|^{s}}\varkappa(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq C\hbar^{n+1-s}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}:2^{k}\leq C/\hbar}2^{k(n+1-s)}=O(1)

provided that
s<n+1
; in which case the sum is geometrically growing.
On the other hand, for
s>n+1
, upon adding a different cutoff to exclude that diagonal, we obtain

\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})\frac{\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)}{|x-y|^{s}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(\hbar 2^{k})^{-s}\int_{|x-y|\leq\hbar 2^{k+1}}\hskip-8.5359pt\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq C\hbar^{n+1-s}

since the series is convergent. This completes the proof of (
4.6
).
∎

Lemma
4.1
has the two following consequences.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a relatively compact open set with a smooth boundary,
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and recall that
f|_{\Omega}=f\mathds{1}_{\Omega}
.
For any
\varkappa:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to[0,1]
with compact support,

\int\frac{|f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}}{2|x-y|^{n}}\varkappa(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Moreover, for any cutoff
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}
,

\int\chi(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1}).

(4.7)

Proof.

We have

|f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}=\big(f(x)-f(y)\big)\big(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y)\big)+f(x)f(y)\big(\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(y)+\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\big).

Then according to Lemma
4.1
(
4.5
),

\int\frac{|f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n}}\varkappa(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int\frac{\big(f(x)-f(y)\big)\big(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y)\big)}{|x-y|^{n}}\varkappa(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(1).

Since
f
is smooth, the first term is controlled by

\displaystyle\int\frac{\varkappa(x,y)}{|x-y|^{n-1}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y<\infty
.

Similarly, by Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int\chi(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})

\displaystyle(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\int\chi(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})(f(x)-f(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}\sqrt{\int\chi(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+2})\int\chi(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})

where we used that
\int\chi(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})(f(x)-f(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+2})
for
f
Lipchitz-continuous.
This inequality implies (
4.7
), otherwise upon dividing by
\sqrt{\int\chi(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}
we obtain a contradiction.

Let us also record that by (
4.6
) and an analogous argument, given a cutoff
\overline{\chi}
, we have

\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})\frac{|f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq\sqrt{\int\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}\sqrt{\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})\frac{|f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{-1}).

Since
\displaystyle\int\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y<\infty
, we conclude that

\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})\frac{|f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{-1}).\qed

(4.8)

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two relatively compact open
sets with smooth boundary such that the
(n-1)
-Hausdorff measure of the
intersection
\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2}
is zero. Then as
\hbar\to 0
,

\int\frac{|(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(y))(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(y))|}{(\hbar+|x-y|)^{n+1}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=o(\log(\hbar^{-1})).

(4.9)

If in fact there exists
\beta<n-1
such that
H^{\beta}(\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2})<+\infty
, then

\int\frac{|(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(y))(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(y))|}{(\hbar+|x-y|)^{n+1}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{O}(1).

(4.10)

Proof.

We claim that as
\eta\to 0
, the integral

\int_{|x-y|\leq\eta}(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(y))(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(y))\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=o(\eta^{1+n}).

(4.11)

Indeed, since the integrand is supported on
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:,\dist(x,\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2})<\eta\}
and

H^{n-1}(\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2})=0
, by a volume
estimate we obtain

\big|\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}:\dist(x,\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2}),\dist(x,y)<\eta\}\big|=o(\eta^{1+n}).

Moreover, for every
\hbar_{0}>0
,

\int_{|x-y|>\hbar_{0}}\frac{(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(y))(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(y))}{|x-y|^{n+1}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq C/\hbar_{0}

The result follows directly form these estimates by a dyadic decomposition; one has for
\hbar\leq\hbar_{0}
,

\eqref{covlog}\leq\sum_{k\leq\log(1/\hbar)}\int_{\hbar 2^{k-1}\leq|x-y|\leq\hbar 2^{k}}\frac{|(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(y))(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(y))|}{|x-y|^{n+1}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar_{0}^{-1})

Using (
4.11
), with
\eta=\hbar 2^{k}
, these integrals are all

o(1)
uniformly for
k\leq\log(\hbar^{-1})
,
\hbar\leq\hbar_{0}
, as
\hbar_{0}\to 0
.
This shows that

\eqref{covlog}=o(\log(\hbar^{-1}))

by choosing
\hbar_{0}
sufficiently small.

Under any positive improvement on the Hausdorff dimension of
\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2}
, one has instead, for some
\epsilon>0
,

\big|\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}:\dist(x,\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2}),\dist(x,y)<\eta\}\big|=o(\eta^{1+n+\epsilon}),

and now the estimates of the dyadic decomposition form a convergent series. This
concludes the proof.
∎

As we already alluded to, the following class of functions plays a key
role when bounding the integral (
4.1
).

Definition 4.4

(Class
\mathcal{F}
)
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, define the class

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{n})=\big\{f:\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}\text{ continuous with compact support};x\mapsto f(x,\xi)\text{ is smooth for }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

\displaystyle\text{and for every }j,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,\big\|\partial_{\xi}^{k}\partial_{x}^{j}f(x,\xi)\big\|\leq C_{j,k}|\xi|^{1-k}\text{ for all }(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\big\}.

We record the following two lemmas without proofs as they are direct consequences of this definition.

Lemma 4.5
.

Functions in
\mathcal{F}
are Lipschitz continuous with respect to
\xi
and there are smooth on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
.
If
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
with
g(x,\xi,0)=0
, then

f(x,\xi)=g(x,\xi,|\xi|)
is in the class
\mathcal{F}
.
We also emphasize that the class
\mathcal{F}
is stable under smooth change of variables on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
which coincide with the identity outside of a compact set.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
a\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
For any cutoff
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, as
\hbar\to 0
,

\int\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})|a(x,\xi)|\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1}).

The last lemma is complemented by the following claim on the zone

\{|\xi|\geq\hbar\}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\leq n+2
. Let
b:\mathbb{R}^{3n}\to\mathbb{R}
have compact
support, smooth with respect to
\xi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
and
such that for every
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\int\frac{\|\partial_{\xi}^{j}b(x,y,\xi)\|}{|x-y|^{n+2-k}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\leq|\xi|^{1-j-k},\qquad\text{for }\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(4.12)

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3n})
be such that, for every
x,y,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\mathds{1}_{|\xi|\leq 1}\leq\chi(x,y,\xi)\leq\mathds{1}_{|\xi|\leq 2}.

Then as
\hbar\to 0
,

\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(1-\chi(x,y,\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))b(x,y,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1-k}).

Proof.

We will prove this claim by decreasing order of the value of
k
: at

k=n+2
, the claim follows from the simple fact that, for any fixed

R>0
, as
\hbar\to 0
, one has

\int_{\hbar\leq|\xi|\leq R}|\xi|^{-n-1}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{-1}).

Suppose now that the claim holds for some value of
k
and let us
prove it for
k-1
; to this end we integrate by parts

\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(1-\chi(x,y,\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))b(x,y,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\\
=i\hbar\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(1-\chi(x,y,\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))\frac{(x-y)\cdot\partial_{\xi}b(x,y,\xi)}{\|x-y\|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\\
-i\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(\partial_{\xi}\chi)(x,y,\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\cdot\frac{(x-y)b(x,y,\xi)}{|x-y|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

Note that the first term in the equation is exactly of the form
given before, where now
B=\frac{(x-y)\cdot\partial_{\xi}b(x,y,\xi)}{\|x-y\|^{2}}
satisfies (
4.12
);
thus

i\hbar\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(1-\chi(x,y,\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))\frac{(x-y)\cdot\partial_{\xi}b(x,y,\xi)}{|x-y|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+2-k}).

It remains to bound

i\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(\partial_{\xi}\chi)(x,y,\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\cdot\frac{(x-y)b(x,y,\xi)}{|x-y|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{O}\left(\int_{\hbar\leq|\xi|\leq 2\hbar}|\xi|^{2-k}\right)=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1-k}).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, we also need an estimate for the commutator between a pseudodifferential operators with smooth, compactly supported symbols and a function with jump discontinuities.
This is to be compared with Proposition
2.6
, albeit simpler.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
a\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3n})

and

\displaystyle K:(x,y)\mapsto\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\xi\cdot(x-y)}{\hbar}}a(x,y,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi.

Then, for any bounded open set
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with smooth boundary,

\big\|[f|_{\Omega},K]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=O(\hbar^{1-n}).

Proof.

By definition, the Hilbert-Schmidt norm,

\displaystyle\|[f|_{\Omega},K]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}a\left(x,y,\xi_{1}\right)\overline{a}\left(y,x,\xi_{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

We make a change of variable
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}
and
\zeta=\frac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2}
so that

\|[f|_{\Omega},K]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}b\left(x,y,\xi,\zeta\right)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

where
b\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n})
.
Using the bound (
4.7
), up to an error
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
, we can introduce a cutoff excluding the diagonal in the previous integral. Then, we perform integrations by parts with respect to
\xi
; we obtain for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\|[f|_{\Omega},K]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\frac{(i\hbar)^{k}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar})e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\frac{(x-y)^{\otimes k}\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{k}b\left(x,y,\xi,\zeta\right)}{|x-y|^{2k}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n}).

We can bound for
(x,y,\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{4n}
with
k=n+2
,

\left|\frac{(x-y)^{\otimes k}\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{k}b\left(x,y,\xi,\zeta,\hbar\right)}{|x-y|^{2k}}\right|\leq\frac{\varkappa(\xi,\zeta)}{|x-y|^{n+2}}

where
\varkappa\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
. Thus, by (
4.8
), we conclude that
\big\|[f|_{\Omega},K]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=O(\hbar^{1-n})
.
∎

4.2
Reduction to an oscillatory integral

Starting from the expression (
4.1
), using the stationary phase
method and certain well-chosen changes of variables, we can prove the following result.

Proposition 4.9
.

Under the assumptions from Section
2.1
, there is a symbol
F\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
(independent of
\Omega
) such that as
\hbar\to 0
,

\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\frac{-1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n}).

Moreover, the principal part of
F
satisfies uniformly as
\xi\to 0
,

F(x,x,\xi)|_{\hbar=0}=\mathrm{c}_{n-1}|\xi||V(x)|^{\frac{n-1}{2}}+\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{2})

(4.13)

with the constant
\mathrm{c}_{n-1}
as in Theorem
1
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Starting from formula (
4.1
), let us explain the main steps of the proof of Proposition
4.9
.

•

Step 1.
As in Section
3.1
, we perform a change of coordinates similar to (
3.7
)

\begin{cases}\lambda=\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}{2},\qquad\xi_{1}=r_{1}\omega_{1}\\
\sigma=\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1},\quad\xi_{2}=r_{2}\omega_{2}\end{cases}\quad\text{with $r_{1},r_{2}>\underline{\mathrm{c}}$, $\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}$}.

(4.14)

This is justified because of the support condition (
4.3
),
which ensures that
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are bounded away from zero. Observe that
\mathds{1}_{\lambda_{1}\leq 0}\mathds{1}_{\lambda_{2}\leq 0}=\mathds{1}_{|\sigma|\leq-2\lambda}
under this change
of variables.
Then, we can perform a stationary phase with respect to the variables

(t_{1},r_{1},t_{2},r_{2})
, keeping
(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)

fixed, and we obtain
an integral of the type

\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\frac{\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}A_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)\mathds{1}_{\{|\sigma|\leq-2\lambda\}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathrm{d}\sigma

(4.15)

where the amplitude
A_{1}\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+2})
is also supported in
\big\{t\in[-\underline{\tau},\underline{\tau}],(x,y)\in\Omega^{\prime 2},|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell},|\lambda|\leq\underline{\ell}\}
.

•

Step 2.
We study the phase
\Psi_{1}
.
This phase vanishes
along the diagonal
\{x=y\}
and it can be factorised into

\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)=(x-y)\cdot\zeta(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{1},\lambda,\sigma)

where
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{4n+2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is smooth and approximated by

\zeta\simeq\sqrt{\lambda-V(x)}(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})
for
x-y
small.
This allows us to show that for
\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
,
x\in\Omega^{\prime}
,
|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell}
fixed, the map
(\omega_{2},\sigma)\mapsto\zeta
is a (smooth) diffeomorphism from a
neighborhood of
(\omega_{1},0)
in
S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
onto
\{\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\zeta|\leq\delta\}
, where
\delta
can be chosen much
larger than
\underline{\ell}
and much smaller than
\underline{\mathrm{c}}
.

We will justify in step 4 that the main contribution to the integral (
4.15
) comes from this region.
Hence, by a change of coordinates, the main term in
\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}
is

\frac{-1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\zeta}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}A_{2}(x,y,\omega,\zeta,\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\{|\sigma(x,y,\zeta,\omega,\lambda)|>-2\lambda>0\}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}\lambda

where
A_{2}\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
,
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth and
\sigma\simeq 2\sqrt{\lambda-V(x)}\,\zeta\cdot\omega
.

•

Step 3.

At this stage, we argue that symbols of the type

(x,y,\xi)\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}A_{2}(x,y,\omega,\xi,\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\{|\sigma(x,y,\xi,\omega,\lambda)|>-2\lambda>0\}}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}\lambda

are in the class
\mathcal{F}
and we prove (
4.13
).

•

Step 4.
It remains to show that the remaining part of the integral (
4.15
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{(x-y)\cdot\zeta(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{1},\lambda,\sigma)}{\hbar}}

\displaystyle(1-\chi_{\delta})\big(\zeta(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{1},\lambda,\sigma)\big)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}

(4.16)

\displaystyle A_{2}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)\mathds{1}_{\{|\sigma|\leq-2\lambda\}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathrm{d}\sigma,

is
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
. Here, as usual
\chi_{\delta}(\cdot)=\chi(\delta\cdot)
, and the function
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
is smooth, equal to
1
on

B(0,\tfrac{\delta}{2})
and to
0
outside
B(0,\delta)
.

If
|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell}
with
\underline{\ell}
small with
respect to
\delta
, then the oscillating phase
(
4.16
) has no stationary point. Thus we expect
(
4.16
) to be rather small. The issue is that the amplitude is not smooth with respect to
(x,y)
.
Nevertheless, we can integrate by parts twice, once with respect to

x
and once with respect to
y
, and this reduces (
4.16
)
(up to an error
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
) to an integral over the (smooth)
boundary of
\Omega
. It remains to bound an integral of the type

\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2(n-1)}}\int_{\{\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\partial\Omega\}\times\{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\partial B\}}e^{i\tfrac{(\hat{x}-\hat{y})\cdot\zeta(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{1},\lambda,\sigma)}{\hbar}}\overline{\chi_{\delta}}(\omega-\omega_{2}))A_{3}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)\mathrm{d}\hat{x}\mathrm{d}\hat{y}\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}

where
A_{3}\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+2})
.
To complete the proof, we argue that for a
\hat{x}\in\partial\Omega
, the phase has non-singular Hessian in one of the pair
(\hat{y},\omega_{1})
or

(\hat{y},\omega_{2})
.
Hence, by a stationary phase argument, this integral is at most of order
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
. This completes the proof.

4.2.1
Step 1: Stationary phase

We make the change of coordinates (
4.14
) (the Jacobian of this change of variable is
(r_{1}r_{2})^{n-1}
for
n\geq 1
) and study the critical point of the phase
\Psi_{0}
in the variables
(t_{1},r_{1},t_{2},r_{2})
. According to (
3.5
) and (
2.2
), we verify that

\displaystyle\partial_{t_{1}}\Psi_{1}(x,y,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},t_{1},t_{2},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t_{1},x,\xi_{1})-\lambda_{1}=V(x)+r_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{1}+\mathcal{O}(t_{1})

(4.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{r_{1}}\Psi_{1}(x,y,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},t_{1},t_{2},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle=\omega_{1}\cdot\big(\partial_{\xi}\varphi(t_{1},x,\xi_{1})-y\big)=\omega_{1}\cdot\big((x-y)+\mathcal{O}(t_{1}^{2})\big)+2t_{1}r_{1}

and similarly for
\partial_{t_{2}}\Psi_{1},\partial_{r_{2}}\Psi_{1}

switching
1\leftarrow 2
and the sign.

Let us first study the first line of (
4.17
). Since
|t_{1}|\leq\underline{\tau}\ll\underline{c}\leq\lambda-\sigma/2-V(x)
, for every
t_{1}
there exists a unique

r_{1}^{c}
solving the first equation, it is a smooth function of all
other parameters, and

r_{1}^{c}|_{t_{1}=0}=\sqrt{\lambda+\sigma/2-V(x)}
. In particular,

r_{1}^{c}\geq\underline{c}
. Now we turn to the
second line of (
4.17
): with
r_{1}=r_{1}^{c}
bounded away from
below, for every
|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell}\ll\underline{c}
there
exists at most one solution for
t_{1}
such that
|t_{1}|\leq\underline{\tau}
; moreover at
y=x
this solution exists and is equal
to
0
.

All in all, for
x-y
small there exists a unique stationary point given by

\displaystyle r_{1}^{c}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\lambda+\sigma/2-V(x)}+O(|x-y|)

(4.18)

\displaystyle t_{1}^{c}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\omega_{1}\cdot(x-y)}{2\sqrt{\lambda+\sigma/2-V(x)}}+\mathcal{O}(|x-y|^{2})

and similarly for
(r_{2}^{c},t_{2}^{c})
replacing
(\lambda+\sigma)\leftarrow(\lambda-\sigma)
and
\omega_{1}\leftarrow\omega_{2}
.
Differentiating (
4.17
) again, we obtain that the Hessian of
\Psi_{1}
with respect to
(t_{1},r_{1},t_{2},r_{2})
is of the form

2\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\star&r_{1}+\mathcal{O}(t_{1})&0&0\\
r_{1}+\mathcal{O}(t_{1})&t_{1}+\mathcal{O}(t_{1}^{2})&0&0\\
0&0&\star&-r_{2}+\mathcal{O}(t_{2})\\
0&0&r_{2}+\mathcal{O}(t_{2})&t_{2}+\mathcal{O}(t_{2}^{2})\end{smallmatrix}\right).

For
r_{1},r_{2}\geq\underline{\mathrm{c}}/4
and
|t_{1}|,|t_{2}|\leq\underline{\tau}
, this Hessian is non-degenerate and its determinant, evaluated at the critical point (
4.18
), is given by

\mathrm{J}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=16\big((\lambda-V(x))^{2}-\sigma^{2}/4\big)+\mathcal{O}(|x-y|).

(4.19)

We introduce a new phase

\Psi_{1}:(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)\mapsto\Psi_{0}(x,y,t_{1}^{c},t_{2}^{c},r_{1}^{c}\omega_{1},r_{2}^{c}\omega_{2},\lambda+\tfrac{\sigma}{2},\lambda-\tfrac{\sigma}{2})

and we denote

R=R(x,\lambda):=\sqrt{\lambda-V(x)}.

(4.20)

By (
4.18
), if
x=y
and
\sigma=0
one has
R=r_{j}^{c}
for

j\in\{1,2\}
as well as

\mathrm{J}=(2R)^{4}
.

We are in position to apply Lemma
A.1
to the integral
(
4.1
), with the variables

(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)
as parameters. There is an amplitude
A_{1}\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+2})
so that

\big\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}\\
=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}A_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)\mathds{1}_{\{|\sigma|\leq-2\lambda\}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\lambda.

(4.21)

Moreover, the principal symbol of
A_{1}
is given as follows, in terms of (
4.18
) and (
4.19
),

A_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)|_{\hbar=0}=\frac{(r_{1}^{c}r_{2}^{c})^{n-1}}{\sqrt{\mathrm{J}}}A_{0}(x,y,r_{1}^{c}\omega_{1},r_{2}^{c}\omega_{2},t_{1}^{c},t_{2}^{c},\lambda+\tfrac{\sigma}{2},\lambda-\tfrac{\sigma}{2}).

The factor
(r_{1}^{c}r_{2}^{c})^{n-1}
comes from the Jacobian of the change
of coordinates.
In particular, according to (
4.2
), at
(y,\sigma)=(x,0)
,

\displaystyle A_{1}(x,x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{R^{2n-2}A_{0}(x,x,R\omega_{1},R\omega_{2},0,0,\lambda,\lambda)}{\sqrt{R^{4}}}=R^{2n-4}\vartheta(V(x)+R^{2})^{2}\chi(\lambda)^{2}/4

\displaystyle=R^{2(n-2)}\mathrm{g}(\lambda)/4,\qquad\qquad x\in\Omega^{\prime},\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1},

(4.22)

where
R
is given by (
4.20
) and
\mathrm{g}:=\vartheta^{2}\chi^{2}
satisfies
\mathrm{g}(0)=0
.
Moreover,
A_{1}
is supported in

\big\{(x,y)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime},|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell},|\lambda|\leq\underline{\ell}\}.

4.2.2
Step 2: Study of the phase
\Psi_{1}

By (
4.18
),
t_{1}^{c}=t_{2}^{c}=0
if
x=y
, then with
\xi_{j}=r_{j}^{c}\omega_{j}
, we have on the diagonal

\Psi_{1}(x,x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)=\varphi(0,x,\xi_{1})-\varphi(0,x,\xi_{2})-(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})\cdot x=0.

Thus there exists a smooth function
\zeta:\mathbb{R}^{2n+2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that we can write

\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma)=(x-y)\cdot\zeta(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\lambda,\sigma).

(4.23)

Moreover, the original phase

\Psi_{1}
is anti-symmetric with respect to the exchange

\xi_{1}\leftrightarrow\xi_{2},t_{1}\leftrightarrow t_{2},\lambda_{1}\leftrightarrow\lambda_{2}
.
From the critical point equation, this implies that
(r_{1}^{c},t_{1}^{c})=(r_{2}^{c},t_{2}^{c})=(r^{c},t^{c})
if
(\omega_{1},\sigma)=(\omega_{2},0)

so that

\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega,\omega,\lambda,0)=\Psi_{0}(x,y,t^{c},t^{c},r^{c}\omega,r^{c}\omega,\lambda,\lambda)=0

Hence

\zeta(x,y,\omega,\omega,\lambda,0)=0,\qquad\text{on }\big\{(x,y)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime},|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell},|\lambda|\leq\underline{\ell}\}.

In particular, with
\mathcal{Z}(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=\zeta(x,x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)
,

\zeta(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=\mathcal{Z}(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)+\mathcal{O}\Big(\sqrt{|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|^{2}+|\sigma|^{2}}|x-y|\Big).

(4.24)

Recall that
|x-y|<\underline{\ell}
in the integral in questions,
so we first study
\mathcal{Z}
instead of
\zeta
. Note that

\mathcal{Z}(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=-\partial_{y}\Psi_{1}\big|_{y=x}=-\partial_{y}\Psi_{0}\big|_{y=x,t_{1}=t_{2}=0,r_{1}=r_{1}^{c},r_{2}=r_{2}^{c}}.

(4.25)

Here we used the property of the critical point for which
\partial_{t_{j}}\Psi_{1}|_{t_{1}^{c},r_{1}^{c},t_{2}^{c},r_{2}^{c}}=\partial_{r_{j}}\Psi_{1}|_{t_{1}^{c},r_{1}^{c},t_{2}^{c},r_{2}^{c}}=0
for
j\in\{1,2\}
and
t_{1}^{c}=t_{2}^{c}=0
if
x=y
. Since
\partial_{y}\Psi_{1}=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}
, by (
4.18
), this yields

\mathcal{Z}(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=r_{1}^{c}\omega_{1}-r_{2}^{c}\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}\sqrt{\lambda-V(x)+\tfrac{\sigma}{2}}-\omega_{2}\sqrt{\lambda-V(x)-\tfrac{\sigma}{2}}.

(4.26)

Linearizing this function for small
\sigma
(here,
|\sigma|\leq 2\underline{\ell}
), we obtain

\mathcal{Z}(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})\big(R+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2})\big)+(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2})\tfrac{\sigma}{4R}\big(1+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2})\big)

with
R\geq\underline{\mathrm{c}}
as in (
4.20
).

We now consider the equation
\mathcal{Z}(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=\xi
for fixed
(x,\omega_{1},\lambda)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times[-\underline{\ell},\underline{\ell}]
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We have

\omega_{2}\big(R-\tfrac{\sigma}{4R}\big)=\omega_{1}\big(R+\tfrac{\sigma}{4R}\big)-\xi+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}).

To solve this equation, we decompose
\omega_{2}=\alpha\omega_{1}+\nu
where
\nu\in\omega_{1}^{\perp}
and
\alpha=\sqrt{1-|\nu|^{2}}\geq 0
, we obtain

\begin{cases}\alpha=1+\tfrac{\sigma}{2R^{2}}-\tfrac{\xi_{1}}{R-\tfrac{\sigma}{4R}}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}),&\text{ where }\xi_{1}=\xi\cdot\omega_{1}\\
\nu=-\tfrac{\xi^{\perp}}{R-\tfrac{\sigma}{4R}}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}),&\text{ where }\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi^{\perp}.\end{cases}

In particular, we have

1=\alpha^{2}+|\nu|^{2}=1+\tfrac{|\xi|^{2}}{(R-\sigma/4R)^{2}}+\tfrac{\sigma}{R^{2}}-\tfrac{2\xi_{1}}{R-\sigma/4R}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2}).

Since
\delta\ll\underline{\mathrm{c}}\leq R
, the last equation
determines
\sigma
for
|\xi|<\delta
, and

\sigma=2R\xi_{1}+\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{2})\qquad\text{and then}\qquad\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}-\xi^{\perp}/R+\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{2}).

To summarize, choosing a small parameter
\delta
with

\underline{\ell}\ll\delta\ll\underline{\mathrm{c}}
, given
(x,\omega_{1},\lambda)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in\delta B_{n}
, the equation

\mathcal{Z}(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=\xi

has a unique smooth solution
(\sigma,\omega_{2})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
.
Moreover, by (
4.26
), we also compute

\partial_{\sigma}\mathcal{Z}=\tfrac{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}{4R}+\mathcal{O}\big(\sigma(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})\big),\qquad\partial_{\omega_{2}}\mathcal{Z}=R\mathrm{I}_{n}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma),

where
\mathrm{I}_{n}
denotes the identity matrix. These derivatives are non-degenerate in a neighbourhood of the previous solution, so according to (
4.24
), the equation
\zeta(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=\xi
, given
(x,\omega_{1},\lambda)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
and
(y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with
|x-y|<\underline{\ell}
,
|\xi|<\delta
, also has a unique smooth solution
(\sigma,\omega_{2})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
with the expansions:

\begin{cases}\sigma=2R\xi_{1}+\mathcal{O}\big(|\xi|(|\xi|+|x-y|)\big)\\
\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}-\xi^{\perp}/R+\mathcal{O}\big(|\xi|(|\xi|+|x-y|)\big)\end{cases},\qquad\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi^{\perp}),\,\xi_{1}=\xi\cdot\omega_{1},\,R=\sqrt{\lambda-V(x)}.

(4.27)

Hence, we can make a change of variable
\xi\in\delta B_{n}\mapsto(\sigma,\omega_{2})
, after which the phase becomes

\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=(x-y)\cdot\xi.

To use this, we must split the integral (
4.21
) in two parts by introducing a cutoff in
\mathcal{Z}
(equivalently in
\xi
after the change of coordinates):

\big\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=I_{1,\hbar}+I_{2,\hbar}

where

\displaystyle I_{1,\hbar}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}A_{1}^{\prime}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\{|\sigma|\leq-2\lambda\}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\lambda,

(4.28)

\displaystyle I_{2,\hbar}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}A_{2}(x,y,\omega_{1},\xi,\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\{|\sigma(x,y,\xi,\omega_{1},\lambda)|\leq-2\lambda\}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\lambda.

We tune a smooth cutoff so that

•

the amplitude
A_{1}^{\prime}
belongs to
S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+2})
with
A_{1}^{\prime}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)=0
on the set

\big\{|\mathcal{Z}\big(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\sigma,\lambda)|\leq\delta\big\}

and
A_{1}^{\prime}
is supported on the set
(x,\lambda)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times[-\underline{\ell},\underline{\ell}]
and
|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell}
with
\underline{\ell}\ll\delta
.

•

the amplitude
A_{2}\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
and it is supported in

(x,\lambda)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R},\;(y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},\;|y-x|+|\lambda|+|\xi|\leq 2\delta\ll\underline{\mathrm{c}}.

Finally, the principal part of
A_{2}
satisfies on the diagonal
x=y

and at
\xi=0
(where
\mathcal{Z}=0
also)

A_{2}(x,x,0,\omega_{1},\lambda)|_{\hbar=0}=A_{1}(x,x,\omega_{1},\omega_{1},\lambda,0)\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}}{\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi^{\perp}}\bigg)\bigg|_{y=x,\xi=0}

since
(\sigma,\omega_{2})=(0,\omega_{1})
when
(y,\xi)=(x,0)
, cf. (
4.27
). Moreover, we compute the Jacobian

\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}}{\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi^{\perp}}\bigg|_{y=x,\xi=0}=\left|\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2R&\ldots&0&\ldots\\
\vdots&\ddots\\
0&&\mathrm{I}_{n-1}/R\\
\vdots&&&\ddots\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right|=2R^{2-n}.

Hence, by (
4.22
) with
R(x,0)=|V(x)|^{1/2}
, we obtain

A_{2}(x,x,0,\omega,0)=R(x,0)^{n-2}\mathrm{g}(0)/2=|V(x)|^{n/2-1}/2,\qquad x\in\Omega^{\prime},\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}.

(4.29)

4.2.3
Step 3: the main symbol is in the class
\mathcal{F}

We now prove the following fact.

Proposition 4.10
.

The symbol

F:(x,y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}\times(0,1]\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}\hskip-14.22636ptA_{2}(x,y,\xi,\omega,\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\{|\sigma(x,y,\xi,\omega,\lambda)|>-2\lambda>0\}}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}\lambda.

is in the class
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
(cf. Definition
4.4
) and it satisfies (
4.13
).

According to (
4.28
) and the previous definition, we can rewrite

I_{2,\hbar}=\frac{-1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}F(x,y,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\xi+\|[f|_{\Omega},K]\|^{2}

where

K:(x,y)\mapsto\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,y,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\xi\quad\text{and}\quad a:\mathbb{R}^{3n}\mapsto\int A_{2}(x,y,\xi,\omega,\lambda)\mathds{1}_{\{\lambda<0\}}\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathrm{d}\omega.

The amplitude
a
belongs to
S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3n})
(
\|a\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}}
are
bounded uniformly in
\hbar
by differentiating under the integral).
Consequently, by Lemma
4.8
,

\|[f|_{\Omega},K]\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})

We will show in the next section (Proposition
4.11
) that the integral
I_{1,\hbar}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
as
\hbar\to 0
. This allows to conclude that

\big\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}F(x,y,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\xi+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})

which completes the proof of Proposition
4.9
.

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

First observe that according to (
4.27
), there exist two smooth functions

\nu:(x,y,\xi,\omega,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}\mapsto\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and
r:(x,y,\xi,\omega,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\sigma=2r\nu\cdot\xi,\qquad(r,\nu)=(R,\omega)+\mathcal{O}\big(|x-y|+|\xi|\big).

(4.30)

Then, we claim that for
\big\{(x,y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}:x\in\Omega^{\prime},|x-y|,|\xi|<2\delta\big\}
, we can make a (smooth) change of coordinates

(\lambda,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\mapsto(\varsigma,\nu)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1},\qquad\varsigma=-\lambda/r

in the integral
F
.
We denote by
\mathrm{J}:(x,y,\xi,\nu,\varsigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{4n+1}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
the corresponding Jacobian.
Observe that if
(y,\xi)=(x,0)
,
\varsigma=-\lambda/\sqrt{\lambda-V(x)}
and, by solving this quadratic equation and choosing the appropriate root, we obtain

\lambda=-\varsigma\sqrt{-V(x)}+\mathcal{O}(\varsigma^{2}),\qquad\omega=\nu.

Thus, in this case,

\mathrm{J}(x,x,0,\nu,\varsigma)=\sqrt{-V(x)}+\mathcal{O}(\varsigma).

(4.31)

Generally,
|\varsigma|\leq\underline{\mathrm{c}}
(as
r\geq\underline{\mathrm{c}}
for
x\in\Omega^{\prime}
and
|y-x|,|\lambda|,|\xi|\leq 2\delta
with
\delta\ll\underline{\mathrm{c}}
), so a similar computation using (
4.30
) shows that
\mathrm{J}(x,y,\xi,\nu,\varsigma)
is non-degenerate.
Hence, this change of coordinates is admissible and there is an amplitude
B\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n+1})
so that

F=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}\hskip-14.22636ptB(x,y,\xi,\nu,\varsigma)\mathds{1}_{\{|\nu\cdot\xi|>\varsigma>0\}}\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}\varsigma.

(4.32)

Moreover, by (
4.30
), the principal part of
B
is given by, if
(y,\xi,\varsigma)=(x,0,0)
with
x\in\Omega^{\prime}
,
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
,

\displaystyle B(x,x,0,\nu,0)|_{\hbar=0}

\displaystyle=A_{2}(x,x,0,\nu,0)|_{\hbar=0}\mathrm{J}(x,x,0,\nu,0)

(4.33)

\displaystyle=|V(x)|^{\frac{n-1}{2}}/2

using (
4.29
) and (
4.31
).

Using spherical coordinates, writing
\xi=R\theta
with
(R,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and making a change of variable
\varsigma\leftarrow R\varsigma
in (
4.32
), we obtain for
(x,y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}

F(x,y,\xi)=R\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}\hskip-14.22636ptB(x,y,\xi,\nu,R\varsigma)\mathds{1}_{\{|\nu\cdot\vartheta|>\varsigma>0\}}\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}\varsigma.

(4.34)

Then, since
B
is smooth, by (
4.33
), the principal part of
F
satisfies on the diagonal
\{x=y\}
,

\displaystyle F(x,x,\xi)

\displaystyle=R\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}\hskip-14.22636pt\big(B(x,x,0,\nu,0)+\mathcal{O}(R)\big)\mathds{1}_{\{|\nu\cdot\vartheta|>\varsigma>0\}}\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}\varsigma

\displaystyle=\frac{|\xi||V(x)|^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}{2}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}|\nu_{1}|\mathrm{d}\nu+\mathcal{O}(|\xi|)\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{c}_{n-1}|\xi||V(x)|^{\frac{n-1}{2}}+\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{2})

uniformly for
x\in\Omega^{\prime}
. Here, we used that for
n\geq 2
,

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}|\nu_{1}|\mathrm{d}\nu=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{n-2}|}{n-1}=|B_{n-1}|

given the relationship between the volume of the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\partial B_{n}
and the unit ball
B_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
(in particular,
|\mathbb{S}^{0}|=|B_{1}|=2
).
In dimension
n=1
, the situation is special as
\nu\in\{\pm 1\}
, so that
\mathrm{c}_{0}=1
.
This establishes (
4.13
).

For
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, let
\mathcal{R}_{\theta}:\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
be the rotation so that
\mathcal{R}_{\theta}^{*}(\theta)=\mathrm{e}_{1}
(the first coordinate vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
).
By (
4.34
), using the invariance under rotation of the Haar measure
\mathrm{d}\nu
on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, we obtain

F(x,y,\xi)=R\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}}\hskip-14.22636ptB(x,y,\xi,\mathcal{R}_{\theta}\nu,R\varsigma)\mathds{1}_{\{|\nu_{1}|>\varsigma>0\}}\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}\varsigma,\qquad\xi=R\theta,\,(R,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}.

(4.35)

This function is clearly smooth in
(x,y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 0\}
.
In fact, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, as
R(\xi)\to 0
,

\big\|\partial_{\xi}^{k}\mathcal{R}_{\theta(\xi)}\big\|=\mathcal{O}_{k}(R^{-k}),\qquad\big\|\partial_{\xi}^{k}R(\xi)\big\|=\mathcal{O}_{k}(R^{1-k}).

Indeed,
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{\theta}
is smooth,
with
\partial_{\xi}R(\xi)=\theta(\xi)
for
\xi\neq 0
and
\big\|\partial_{\xi}^{k}\theta(\xi)\big\|=\mathcal{O}_{k}(R^{-k})
.
Thus, by differentiating (
4.35
) under the integral. we conclude that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\big\|\partial_{\xi}^{k}F(x,y,\xi)\big\|=\mathcal{O}_{k}(R^{1-k}).

as
R(\xi)\to 0
.
This completes the proof that
F\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
∎

4.2.4
Step 4: Control of
I_{1,\hbar}

At this stage, it remains to show that
I_{1,\hbar}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
, where we recall that
I_{1,\hbar}
is given by (
4.28
). By (
4.28
), we can work with
(\sigma,\lambda)
fixed and it suffices to obtain the following bounds (We drop the dependency of
\Psi_{1},\mathcal{Z},
etc on
(\sigma,\lambda)
for notational convenience. The proof of Proposition
4.11
relies on the stationary phase as formulated in Proposition
A.1
which allows to deal with the parameters
(\sigma,\lambda)
in a uniform way.).

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
(\Psi_{1},\mathcal{Z})
be as in Section
4.2.2
and assume that
A\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n})
is supported on the set

\big\{x\in\Omega^{\prime},|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell},|\mathcal{Z}\big(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})|>\delta\big\}
with
\underline{\ell}\ll\delta\ll 1
.
Then, as
\hbar\to 0
,

\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\hskip-28.45274pte^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}A(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n}).

Proof.

Expanding the square, we need to deal with two integrals of the type

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}A_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}

(4.36)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}A_{2}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}

(4.37)

where the amplitudes
A_{1},A_{2}
belong to
S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n})
. There is a
minus sign, but we do not expect a cancelation in this regime.

According to (
4.25
),

\partial_{y}\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=-\mathcal{Z}(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})+\mathcal{O}(x-y).

(4.38)

Hence, if
A\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n})
is supported in

\big\{|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell},|\mathcal{Z}\big(x,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})|>\delta\big\}
with
\underline{\ell}\ll\delta\ll\underline{\mathrm{c}}
,

\partial_{y}\Psi_{1}
does not vanish on
\supp(A)

and we can write

A(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}J(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\cdot\partial_{y}\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}),

where
J:\mathbb{R}^{4n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is also in
S^{1}
with the same support as

A
.

In these circumstances, we can repeat this procedure and integrate by parts with respect to
y

arbitrarily often in (
4.36
), and obtain

\eqref{int1}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty}).

For (
4.37
), we integrate by parts once and find
that there are
B_{1},B_{2}\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n})
so that

(
4.37
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n-1}}\int_{\{\hat{y}\in\partial\Omega\}}\hskip-14.22636pte^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(x,\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}B_{1}(x,\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\hat{y}\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}

(4.39)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2n-1}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}B_{2}(x,y,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(x)\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}.

(4.40)

Here,
B_{1}(\cdot)=\nu_{\Omega}(\hat{y})\cdot J(\cdot)A_{2}(\cdot)

where
\nu_{\Omega}
is the exterior normal to
\partial\Omega
, and we
integrate the
variable
\hat{y}
with respect to the volume
measure over
\partial\Omega
– it is worth remembering here that

\Omega
has a smooth boundary.

The integral (
4.40
) has the same form as the original integral
(
4.37
) with an extra power of
\hbar
.
Thus, if we show that
\eqref{int3}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{-n+1})
, by induction, we can conclude that also
\eqref{int2}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{-n+1})
.

Then we consider the integral (
4.39
). Similarly to (
4.25
), the phase
\partial_{x}\Psi_{1}
does not vanish on
\supp(B_{1})
. Thus, integrating by part with respect to
x
, we obtain

\eqref{int3}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2(n-1)}}\int_{\{\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\partial\Omega\}}\hskip-14.22636pte^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}B_{3}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathrm{d}\hat{x}\mathrm{d}\hat{y}\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}+\frac{Q_{\hbar}}{2\pi\hbar}

(4.41)

where
B_{3}\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n})
and
Q_{\hbar}
is an integral of the same type as (
4.39
).
Thus, by the same reasoning as above, it suffices to show that the
boundary integral in (
4.41
) is
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
to conclude that the original integral is also of order
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
.

In dimension
n=1
, obviously
\eqref{int5}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, so this reasoning already shows that the integral in question is bounded, as required.

In what follows, we assume that
n\geq 2
and focus on the boundary
integral (
4.41
) for a fixed
\hat{x}\in\partial\Omega
. Recall
that the amplitude
B_{3}
belongs to
S^{1}
and it is supported on

\big\{\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\partial\Omega:|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|\leq\underline{\ell},|\mathcal{Z}\big(\hat{x},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})|>\delta\big\}.

Let
\Pi_{\hat{x}}
denote the orthogonal projection on
T_{\hat{x}}(\partial\Omega)
. On the above set (
\underline{\ell}
is small),

(\hat{x}-\hat{y})=\Pi_{\hat{x}}(\hat{x}-\hat{y})+\mathcal{O}\big(|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|^{2}\big)

then, by (
4.23
), the phase satisfies

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=(\hat{x}-\hat{y})\cdot\mathcal{Z}(\hat{x},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})+\mathcal{O}\big(|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|^{2}\big)

(4.42)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\hat{x}}(\hat{x}-\hat{y})\cdot\Pi_{\hat{x}}\mathcal{Z}(\hat{x},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})+\mathcal{O}\big(|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|^{2}\big).

We split the integral (
4.41
) in several parts depending on
\Pi_{\hat{x}}^{\perp}(\omega_{j})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
.
Let

\mathcal{S}_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{\pm}:=\big\{\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}:\pm\Pi_{\hat{x}}^{\perp}(\omega)>\ell\big\}

and
\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{j}:\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\to[0,1]
for

j\in\{0,+,-\}
be smooth cutoffs such that

\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{\hat{x},2\underline{\ell}}^{\pm}}\leq\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{\pm}\leq\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{\pm}}\qquad\qquad\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{0}+\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{-}+\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{+}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}.

We first consider the integrals

(\hat{x},\omega_{2})\mapsto\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2(n-1)}}\int\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{\pm}(\omega_{1})e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}B_{3}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathrm{d}\hat{y}\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}.

(4.43)

Since the map
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\ni\omega\mapsto\Pi_{\hat{x}}\omega
is a diffeomorphism
from
\mathcal{S}_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{\pm}
to its image, we can make a change of variables

\hat{y}\to u=\Pi_{\hat{x}}(\hat{y}-\hat{x}),\qquad\omega_{1}\to v=\Pi_{\hat{x}}(\omega_{1}),

in (
4.43
).
We obtain

\eqref{comm9}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2(n-1)}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{2}(\hat{x},\omega_{2},u,v)}{\hbar}}B_{4}(\hat{x},\omega_{2},u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v

where the amplitude
B_{4}
belongs to
S^{1}
and the new phase
\Psi_{2}
satisfies, by (
4.42
) and (
4.26
),

\Psi_{2}(\hat{x},\omega_{2},u,v)=u\cdot\big(R_{+}v-R_{-}\Pi_{\hat{x}}(\omega_{2})\big)+\mathcal{O}(u^{2}),\qquad R_{\pm}=\sqrt{\lambda-V(\hat{x})\pm\sigma}.

Any critical point of
\Psi_{2}
with respect to
(u,v)
satisfies
u=0
and the Hessian is non-degenerate with determinant
R_{+}^{2(n-1)}
and
R_{\pm}\geq\sqrt{\underline{\mathrm{c}}}

(there is at most one critical point given by
v=\Pi_{\hat{x}}(\omega_{2})R_{-}/R_{+}
).
Thus, by Proposition
A.1
with
d=2(n-1)
, one has the
following estimate, uniformly in
(\hat{x},\omega_{2})\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
(as well as the auxiliary parameters
(\sigma,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
):

\eqref{comm9}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n-1}).

By the same argument, with the same uniformity,

\int\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{\pm}(\omega_{2})e^{i\frac{\Psi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}B_{3}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathrm{d}\hat{y}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n-1}).

Hence, we are left to deal with the integral

\hat{x}\mapsto\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{2(n-1)}}\int\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{0}(\omega_{1})\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{0}(\omega_{2})e^{i\frac{\Psi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}B_{3}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathrm{d}\hat{y}\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}.

Since
{\hat{x}}\mapsto\Pi_{\hat{x}}
is smooth (we assume that
\partial\Omega
is smooth) and
\partial_{\hat{y}}=\Pi_{\hat{y}}(\partial_{y})+\mathcal{O}(x-y)
, according to (
4.38
),

\partial_{\hat{y}}\Psi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\Pi_{\hat{x}}\mathcal{Z}(\hat{x},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\underline{\ell})\qquad\text{for $\big\{\hat{y}\in\partial\Omega:|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|\leq\underline{\ell}\big\}$}.

Thus, on the support of the previous integral,
\big\{|\Pi_{\hat{x}}^{\perp}(\omega_{1})|,|\Pi_{\hat{x}}^{\perp}(\omega_{2})|\leq\underline{\ell},|\mathcal{Z}\big(\hat{x},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})|>\delta\big\}
with
\underline{\ell}\ll\delta
.
Since
\mathcal{Z}
is a linear combination of
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
, see (
4.24
), we have
\Pi_{\hat{x}}\mathcal{Z}(\hat{x},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\mathcal{Z}(\hat{x},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\underline{\ell})
and then

\big|\partial_{\hat{y}}\Psi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\big|\geq\delta/2.

This shows that the phase has no critical point in the previous integral. Hence, by Lemma
A.1
(non-stationary phase version), we conclude that
uniformly in
\hat{x}\in\partial\Omega
(as well as the auxiliary parameters
(\sigma,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
),

\int\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{0}(\omega_{1})\chi_{\hat{x},\underline{\ell}}^{0}(\omega_{2})e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})}{\hbar}}B_{3}(\hat{x},\hat{y},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathrm{d}\hat{y}\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty}).

Altogether, this establishes that the integrals (
4.41
), (
4.39
) as well as the original integral are all
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
with the required uniformity in
(\sigma,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Going back to (
4.28
), we conclude that
I_{1,\hbar}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
and this also finalizes the proof of Proposition
4.9
.
∎

4.3
Case of a contractible open set

It remains to study integrals of the form given by the right-hand-side
of Proposition
4.9
. We first do so in the case where

\Omega
is topologically simple: if it is diffeomorphic to the unit
ball, then we can proceed by a change of variables.

We denote
B=B_{n}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|x|<1\}
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded open set with a smooth
boundary; suppose that there is a
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\varphi^{-1}(\Omega)=B
.
Let
F\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
according to Definition
4.4
and assume that as
r\to 0
,

F(x,x,r\omega)=rR(x)+\mathcal{O}(r^{2})

(4.44)

uniformly for
(x,\omega)\in\Omega^{\prime}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
.
Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and denote
\mathrm{f}=f\mathds{1}_{\Omega}
.
Then, as
\hbar\to 0
,

\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=(2\pi\hbar)^{n+1}\bigg(\frac{\log\hbar^{-1}}{-\pi^{2}}\int_{\partial\Omega}R(\hat{x})f(\hat{x})^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{x}+\mathcal{O}(1)\bigg)

where
\mathrm{d}\hat{x}
denotes the volume measure on the boundary
\partial\Omega
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\varphi=\mathrm{I}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega^{\prime}
. Throughout this section, we denote

L_{\hbar}:=\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

(4.45)

The proof of Proposition
4.12
will be divided in three steps.

•

In Section
4.3.1
, using the map
\varphi
as a change of coordinates, we show that we can reduce (
4.45
) to the case where
\Omega=B
.

•

In Section
4.3.2
, we obtain the asymptotics of
(
4.45
) when
\Omega=B
by using the expression of the
Fourier transform
\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}
in terms of a Bessel function, cf. Lemma
4.16
.

•

Combining these results, we complete the proof in Section
4.3.3
.

These computations rely importantly on the estimates from Section
4.1
for integrals involving
\mathds{1}_{\Omega}
.

4.3.1
Change of variables

Proposition 4.13
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.12
, define a map

\Phi:(x,y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}\mapsto\big(\varphi(x),\varphi(y),D\varphi(\tfrac{x+y}{2})^{-*}\xi\big)

and let
G:=\mathrm{J}_{\Phi}\,F\circ\Phi
. Then
G
is also in the class
\mathcal{F}

and, as
\hbar\to 0
,

\displaystyle\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}G(x,y,\xi)(\mathrm{g}_{B}(x)-\mathrm{g}_{B}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})

where
\mathrm{g}_{B}=f|_{\Omega}(\varphi)=f(\varphi)\mathds{1}_{B}
.

Proof.

By construction, the map
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{3n}\to\mathbb{R}^{3n}
is a
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism and it is straightforward to check that
G
is also in the class
\mathcal{F}
, cf. Definition
4.4
(since
\varphi=\mathrm{I}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega^{\prime}
,
G
has compact support).
In particular, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
k\leq n+3
,

\big\|\partial_{\xi}^{k}G(x,y,\xi)\big\|\leq|\xi|^{1-k}\varkappa_{1}(\xi)\varkappa_{2}(x,y)

(4.46)

where
\varkappa_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\varkappa_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.

By a Taylor expansion, we have for
(x,y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}
,

\big(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\big)\cdot D\varphi(\tfrac{x+y}{2})^{-*}\xi=(x-y)\cdot\xi+Q(\tfrac{x+y}{2},x-y)\xi

where
Q:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
C^{\infty}_{c}
and vanishes at zero
along with its two first derivatives:

|Q(v,u)|\leq C|u|^{3}.

(4.47)

Let us denote

\breve{G}(\tfrac{x+y}{2},x-y,\xi)=G(x,y,\xi),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Gamma(\tfrac{x+y}{2},x-y)=(\mathrm{g}_{B}(x)-\mathrm{g}_{B}(y))^{2}.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be equal to 1 near
0
. By a change of variable using
\Phi
.

\displaystyle\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=

\displaystyle\int e^{i\frac{u\cdot\xi+Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)\Gamma(v,u)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}u

(4.48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}G(x,y,\xi)(\mathrm{g}_{B}(x)-\mathrm{g}_{B}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+I_{\hbar}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})

where

I_{\hbar}=\int(1-\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}\Big(e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big)\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)\Gamma(v,u)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}u.

Here, we used Lemma
4.6
together with the fact that
G\in\mathcal{F}
to introduce the cutoff
1-\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})
in
the integral (
4.48
), up to an error
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})
.

Now, our goal is now to show that
I_{\hbar}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})
as
\hbar\to 0
.
The argument is similar to the proof of Lemma
4.7
, albeit the
induction process is more technical. We will integrate by parts
repeatedly in the variable
u
, stopping every time a derivative hits
either
\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})
or
e^{i\frac{Q}{\hbar}}
. To this
end, we introduce the following integrals for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
k\leq n+2
:

\displaystyle J_{k,\hbar}

\displaystyle=2i\int e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}u\cdot\partial_{\xi}\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\Big(e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big)\frac{u^{\otimes k}\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{k}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)}{|u|^{2(k+1)}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v\,,

\displaystyle I_{k,\hbar}

\displaystyle=\int(1-\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))e^{i\frac{u\xi+Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}\frac{Q(v,u)u}{|u|^{2}}\frac{u^{\otimes k}\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{k}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)}{|u|^{2k}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v\,.

Provided we can show that all these integrals are finite, one has

\displaystyle I_{\hbar}

(4.49)

\displaystyle=i\hbar\int e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}\frac{u\cdot\partial_{\xi}}{|u|^{2}}\left\{(1-\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))\Big(e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big)\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)\right\}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=i\hbar\int(1-\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}\Big(e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big)\frac{u\cdot\partial_{\xi}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)}{|u|^{2}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v-I_{0,\hbar}-J_{0,\hbar}

\displaystyle=(i\hbar)^{2}\int(1-\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}\Big(e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big)\frac{u^{\otimes 2}\cdot\partial^{2}_{\xi}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)}{|u|^{4}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v-I_{0,\hbar}-J_{0,\hbar}-i\hbar(I_{1,\hbar}+J_{1,\hbar})

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle=(i\hbar)^{n+3}\int(1-\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}))e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}\Big(e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big)\frac{u^{\otimes(n+3)}\cdot\partial^{n+3}_{\xi}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)}{|u|^{2(n+3)}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v-\sum_{k=0}^{n+2}(i\hbar)^{k}(I_{k,\hbar}+J_{k,\hbar}).

Let us first bound the
J_{k,\hbar}
integrals.
Using (
4.46
) and that
\Big|e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big|\leq\tfrac{C}{\hbar}|u|^{3}|\xi|

for
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\leq n+2
,

\displaystyle|J_{k,\hbar}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\hbar}\int|\xi|\big|\partial_{\xi}\chi(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\big|\frac{\|\partial_{\xi}^{k}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)\|}{|u|^{k-2}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle\leq C\hbar^{n+1-k}\int|\partial_{\xi}\chi(\xi)||\xi|^{2-k}\frac{(\mathrm{g}_{B}(x)-\mathrm{g}_{B}(y))^{2}}{|x-y|^{k-2}}\varkappa_{2}(x,y)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

where we rescaled
\frac{\xi}{\hbar}\leftarrow\xi
since
|\partial_{\xi}\chi|
has compact support in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
.
By Proposition
4.2
, these integrals are finite so that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
k\leq n+2
,

J_{k,\hbar}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1+n-k}).

(4.50)

To estimate
I_{k,\hbar}
, we reverse the change of variables
\Phi
,
and obtain

I_{k,\hbar}=\int(1-\chi(\tfrac{A(x,y)\xi}{\hbar}))e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}L_{k}(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\xi

(4.51)

where
A:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to{\rm GL}_{n}
is a smooth map, and
L_{k}
is the image by
\Phi^{-1}
of the
function

(x,y,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{3n}\mapsto\frac{Q(\frac{x+y}{2},x-y)(x-y)}{|x-y|^{2}}\frac{(x-y)^{\otimes k}\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{k}\breve{G}(x,y,\xi)}{|x-y|^{2k}}|D\phi(x)||D\phi(y)|\det(A(x,y))^{-1}.

Using (
4.46
) and (
4.47
), together with the fact
that
\Phi
maps the diagonal
x=y
to itself, we verify that for
every
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\big|\partial_{\xi}^{j}L_{k}(x,y,\xi)\big|\leq C|x-y|^{2-k}|\xi|^{1-k-j}\varkappa(\xi,x,y).

where
\varkappa
is compactly supported in a neighbourhood of

x=y
. Hence, by Proposition
4.7
, for every
k\geq n+2
,

I_{k,\hbar}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1+n-k}).

Going back to (
4.49
) and using the previous estimates with
k=0
, we obtain

I_{\hbar}=i\hbar\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}\Big(e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big)\frac{u\cdot\partial_{\xi}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)}{|u|^{2}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1+n}).

The leading term is of the form as
I_{\hbar}
, so we can repeat the process. By induction, after
k\in\mathbb{N}
integrations by part, we obtain

I_{\hbar}=(i\hbar)^{k}\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}\Big(e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big)\frac{u^{\otimes k}\cdot\partial_{\xi}^{k}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)}{|u|^{2k}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v-\sum_{j<k}(i\hbar)^{j}\big(I_{j,\hbar}+J_{j,\hbar}\big).

The error terms are all controlled as above. Hence, taking
k=n+3
, we obtain

\displaystyle|I_{\hbar}|

\displaystyle\leq\hbar^{n+3}\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})e^{i\frac{u\xi}{\hbar}}\Big|e^{i\frac{Q(v,u)\xi}{\hbar}}-1\Big|\frac{\|\partial_{\xi}^{n+3}\breve{G}(v,u,\xi)\|}{|u|^{n+3}}\Gamma(u,v)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1+n})

\displaystyle\leq C\hbar^{n+2}\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})|\xi|^{-1-n}\frac{(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}}{|x-y|^{n}}\varkappa_{2}(x,y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\xi+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1+n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1+n})

where we used again Proposition
4.2
to bound the last integral.

In the end, going back to formula (
4.48
), we conclude that

\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}G(x,y,\xi)(\mathrm{g}_{B}(x)-\mathrm{g}_{B}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+O(\hbar^{n+1})

as claimed.
∎

4.3.2
Asymptotics in case of the ball

The goal of this section is to obtain the following asymptotics.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
F\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
and denote

q(r)=\frac{1}{r}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}F(\omega,\omega,r\omega)f(\omega)^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega,\qquad r>0.

(4.52)

This function is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, with compact support, and we assume that
q(r)\to Q
as
r\to 0
and that

\int_{0}^{1}\bigg|\frac{q(r)-Q}{r}\bigg|\mathrm{d}r<\infty.

(4.53)

Then, by (
4.45
) with
\Omega=B
,

L_{\hbar}=-4\hbar^{n+1}\big((2\pi)^{n-1}Q\log\hbar^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(1)\big).

We first simplify the integral
L_{\hbar}
of (
4.45
), using the estimates from Section
4.1
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
F\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
g(x,\xi):=F(x,x,\xi)f(x)^{2}
for
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
Then
g\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

L_{\hbar}=-2\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}g(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathds{1}_{B}(x)\mathds{1}_{B}(y)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1}).

Proof.

By symmetry of the integral in question, we can assume that

L_{\hbar}=2\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(\mathrm{f}_{B}(x)-\mathrm{f}_{B}(y))\mathrm{f}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Then, we can write

\displaystyle\frac{L_{\hbar}}{2}=\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)\big(f(x)-f(y)\big)\mathrm{f}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(4.54)

\displaystyle\quad+\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}G(x,y,\xi)\mathds{1}_{B}(x)\mathds{1}_{B^{c}}(y)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(4.55)

where
G(x,y,\xi):=F(x,y,\xi)f(x)f(y)
.

We begin by showing that (
4.54
) is negligible.
Since
(x,y)\mapsto F(x,y,\xi)
is
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, by a Taylor expansion,

\big(f(x)-f(y)\big)F(x,y,\xi)=(x-y)\cdot a(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)+b(x,y,\xi)

where
a\in\mathcal{F}
,
b\in\mathcal{F}
and

a(x,\xi)=\partial f(x)F(x,x,\xi),\qquad b(x,y,\xi)=\mathcal{O}\big(|x-y|^{3}\big).

In particular, the function
(x,y,\xi)\mapsto b(x,y,\xi)\mathrm{f}_{B}(x)
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
4.7
. This implies that

\eqref{Ired1}=2\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(x-y)\cdot a(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathrm{f}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})

Then, we perform an integration by part with respect to
\xi
and a change of variables
\frac{x+y}{2}\leftarrow v
,
(x-y)\leftarrow u
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(x-y)\cdot a(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathrm{f}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=(i\hbar)\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a^{\prime}(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathrm{f}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=(i\hbar)\int e^{i\frac{u\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a^{\prime}(v,\xi)\mathrm{f}_{B}(v+\tfrac{u}{2})\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v

where
a^{\prime}=\operatorname{div}_{\xi}a
is
L^{\infty}
with compact support.
Using the Fourier transform, computing the integral over
u
, and then over
v
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}(x-y)\cdot a(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathrm{f}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=i\hbar(2\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}\int e^{-i\frac{v\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a^{\prime}(v,\xi)\overline{\widehat{\mathrm{f}_{B}}(\tfrac{2\xi}{\hbar})}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=i\hbar(2\pi)^{n}\int\widehat{a^{\prime}}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar},\xi)\overline{\widehat{\mathrm{f}_{B}}(\tfrac{2\xi}{\hbar})}\mathrm{d}\xi

(4.56)

where for
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with
\xi\neq 0
,

\widehat{a^{\prime}}(\zeta,\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\int e^{-iv\cdot\zeta}a^{\prime}(v,\xi)\mathrm{d}v=\operatorname{div}_{\xi}\bigg(\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\int e^{-iv\cdot\zeta}a(v,\xi)\mathrm{d}v\bigg).

Since
a\in\mathcal{F}
,
\zeta\mapsto\widehat{a^{\prime}}(\zeta,\xi)
is in the Schwartz class for
\xi\neq 0
and
\xi\mapsto\widehat{a^{\prime}}(\zeta,\xi)
is
L^{\infty}
.
In the end, by scaling and Cauchy-Schwarz,

\int\widehat{a^{\prime}}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar},\xi)\overline{\widehat{\mathrm{f}_{B}}(\tfrac{2\xi}{\hbar})}\mathrm{d}\xi=\mathcal{O}\big(\hbar^{n}\|a^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}\times L^{\infty}}\|\mathrm{f}_{B}\|_{L^{2}}\big),

and we conclude that
\eqref{Ired1}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})
.

Going back to (
4.55
), we have

G(x,y,\xi)=g(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)+e(x,y,\xi)\qquad\text{where $g\in\mathcal{F}$, $e\in\mathcal{F}$ and }e(x,y,\xi)=\mathcal{O}\big(|x-y|^{2}\big),

so that the function

(x,y,\xi)\mapsto e(x,y,\xi)\mathds{1}_{B}(x)\mathds{1}_{B^{c}}(y)

satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
4.7
– the condition (
4.12
) for
j\leq n+2
follow directly from (
4.5
) with
\Omega=B
.
Altogether, this implies that

\frac{L_{\hbar}}{2}=\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}g(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathds{1}_{B}(x)\mathds{1}_{B^{c}}(y)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1}).

(4.57)

Moreover, as in (
4.56
), we have

\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}g(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathds{1}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=(2\pi)^{n}\int\widehat{g}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar},\xi)\overline{\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\tfrac{2\xi}{\hbar})}\mathrm{d}\xi

where for
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\mapsto\widehat{g}(\zeta,\xi)
satisfies
|\widehat{g}(\zeta,\xi)|\leq|\xi|\varkappa(\zeta)
where
\varkappa
is in the Schwartz class; since
g\in\mathcal{F}
. Using this bound and the fact that
\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}\in L^{\infty}
, we obtain

\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}g(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\xi)\mathds{1}_{B}(x)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1}).

Hence, the claim follows from (
4.57
).
∎

The proof relies on the following explicit asymptotics.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\mathrm{J}_{\nu}
denote the Bessel function of the first kind for

\nu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. As
\xi\to+\infty
,

\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\xi)=\frac{\mathrm{J}_{n/2}(|\xi|)}{|\xi|^{n/2}}=\frac{2\cos(|\xi|+c_{n})+\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{-1})}{\sqrt{2\pi}|\xi|^{(n+1)/2}}.

(4.58)

Proof.

The relationship between Bessel functions and the Fourier transform of

\mathds{1}_{B}
where
B
is the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is classical: see
e.g.
[
19
]
, formula (A.5).
The asymptotics of Bessel functions are also classical, see
[
49
,
formula (10.17.3)
]
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.14
.

Starting from Lemma
4.15
and making a change of variables
\frac{x+y}{2}\leftarrow v
,
(x-y)\leftarrow u
, we have

L_{\hbar}=-2\int e^{i\frac{u\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}g(v,\xi)\mathds{1}_{B}(v+\tfrac{u}{2})\mathds{1}_{B}(v-\tfrac{u}{2})\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1}).

Since
g\in\mathcal{F}
, by Lemma
4.6
, we can introduce a cutoff
\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})
in the previous integral, up to an error
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})
.
Moreover, computing this Fourier transform as a convolution (with our convention,
(2\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}\widehat{uv}=\widehat{u}*\widehat{v}
for functions
u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
),

\int e^{i\frac{u\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}\mathds{1}_{B}(v+\tfrac{u}{2})\mathds{1}_{B}(v-\tfrac{u}{2})\mathrm{d}u=2^{n}\int e^{-2iv\cdot\zeta}\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\zeta+\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar}-\zeta)\mathrm{d}\zeta=\int e^{-iv\cdot\zeta}\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\tfrac{\zeta}{2}+\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\tfrac{\zeta}{2}-\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\mathrm{d}\zeta

This implies that

L_{\hbar}=-2\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})e^{-iv\cdot\zeta}g(v,\xi)\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\tfrac{\zeta}{2}+\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\tfrac{\zeta}{2}-\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\xi+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1}).

This integral is well-defined since
\mathds{1}_{B}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
g
has compact support on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
Moreover we can take the Fourier transform of the smooth function
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto g(v,\xi)
for a fixed
\xi
, we obtain

\displaystyle L_{\hbar}=-2(2\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\widehat{g}(\zeta,\xi)\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\tfrac{\zeta}{2}+\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\tfrac{\zeta}{2}-\tfrac{\xi}{\hbar})\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\xi+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})

where for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
r>0
,

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\widehat{g}(\zeta,\xi)|\leq\frac{C_{k}}{(1+|\zeta|^{2})^{k}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\big\|\widehat{g}(\cdot,r\omega)\big\|_{L^{1}}\leq Cr.

(4.59)

Now, using the asymptotics (
4.58
), for every
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\xi=r\omega
with
