(r,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, as
r\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\zeta+\xi)\widehat{\mathds{1}_{B}}(\zeta-\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\cos(r+\omega\cdot\zeta+c_{n})\cos(r-\omega\cdot\zeta+c_{n})+\mathcal{O}(r^{-1})}{\pi r^{n+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos(2r+2c_{n})+\cos(2\zeta\cdot\omega)+\mathcal{O}(r^{-1})}{\pi r^{n+1}}.

The error term is uniform for
|\zeta|\leq r^{\alpha}
for any
0<\alpha<1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
,
so by (
4.59
), we can substitute these asymptotics into the previous formula for
L_{\hbar}
(up to a negligible error).
This yields 3 terms,

L_{\hbar}=-\tfrac{1}{\pi^{2}}(2\pi\hbar)^{n+1}\big(I_{1,\hbar}+I_{2,\hbar}+I_{3,\hbar}\big)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})

(4.60)

where

\displaystyle L_{1,\hbar}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})\widehat{g}(\zeta,r\omega)\cos(\zeta\cdot\omega)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle L_{2,\hbar}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})\widehat{g}(\zeta,r\omega)\cos(2r/\hbar+2c_{n})\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\omega

and
L_{3,\hbar}
is controlled using (
4.59
) as

L_{3,\hbar}=\mathcal{O}\bigg(\hbar\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})\sup_{\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\big\|\widehat{g}(\cdot,r\omega)\big\|_{L^{1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{3}}\bigg)=\mathcal{O}\bigg(\hbar\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}\bigg).

Then, since
\int\overline{\chi}(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}<\infty
,

L_{3,\hbar}=\mathcal{O}(1).

(4.61)

We now turn to the highly oscillating term
L_{2,\hbar}
that we can rewrite (by Fourier’s inversion formula)

L_{2,\hbar}=\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})g(0,r\omega)\cos(2r/\hbar+2c_{n})\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}\mathrm{d}\omega.

Let
j(r):=\int g(0,r\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega
, which we may view as an even function in
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R})
. So

L_{2,\hbar}=\Re\bigg(e^{2ic_{n}}\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})j(r)e^{i\tfrac{2r}{\hbar}}\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}\bigg).

and we can make an integration by parts,

L_{2,\hbar}=-\frac{\hbar}{2}\Im\bigg(e^{2ic_{n}}\int\partial_{r}\big\{\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})j(r)\tfrac{1}{r^{2}}\big\}e^{i\tfrac{2r}{\hbar}}\mathrm{d}r\bigg).

Then, we control
L_{2,\hbar}
as follows,

\left|\int\partial_{r}\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})j(r)r^{-2}\mathrm{d}r\right|\leq C\int|\chi^{\prime}(r)|\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r}<\infty,

similarly

\hbar\int\left|\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})j(r)r^{-3}\right|\mathrm{d}r\leq C\hbar\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}=C\int\overline{\chi}(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}<\infty

and

\hbar\int\left|\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})j^{\prime}(r)r^{-2}\right|\mathrm{d}r\leq C\int\overline{\chi}(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}<\infty.

We conclude that

L_{2,\hbar}=\mathcal{O}(1).

(4.62)

We finally turn to the main
L_{1,\hbar}
which we compute using Fourier’s inversion formula,

L_{1,\hbar}=\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})g(\omega,r\omega)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\omega

where we used the invariance of the Haar measure
\mathrm{d}\omega
on
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
.
Using the notation (
4.52
), this yields

L_{1,\hbar}=\int\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})q(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r}.

Since
q(r)\to Q
as
r\to 0
, if
Q\neq 0
, this integral cannot be bounded as
\hbar\to 0
. However, it diverges logarithmically under the assumption (
4.53
).
Indeed, we can split if
C>0
is sufficiently large (depending only on the cutoff),

L_{1,\hbar}=Q\bigg(\int_{C\hbar}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r}+\int_{0}^{C\hbar}\overline{\chi}(\tfrac{r}{\hbar})\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r}\bigg)+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{q(r)}{r}\mathrm{d}r+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{q(r)-Q}{r}\right|\mathrm{d}r\bigg)

The last three terms are
\mathcal{O}(1)
since
\int_{0}^{C}\overline{\chi}(r)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r}<\infty
,
q\in C_{c}
and (
4.53
) holds. This shows that

L_{1,\hbar}=Q\log\hbar^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(1).

Combining this asymptotics with (
4.61
), (
4.62
) into (
4.60
), we conclude that as
\hbar\to 0
,

L_{\hbar}=-\tfrac{1}{\pi^{2}}(2\pi\hbar)^{n+1}\big(Q\log\hbar^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(1)\big).\qed

4.3.3
Proof of Proposition
4.12

We now put together the different steps of the proof of Proposition
4.12
.
Recall that
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism such that
\varphi^{-1}(\Omega)=B
.
First by Proposition
4.13
with
g=f(\varphi)

\displaystyle L_{\hbar}

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}G(x,y,\xi)(g|_{B}(x)-g|_{B}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n+1})

and on the diagonal,

G(x,x,\xi)=F\big(\varphi(x),\varphi(x),D\varphi(x)^{-*}\xi\big)\mathrm{J}_{\varphi}(x),\qquad(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

Here we used that by construction,
\mathrm{J}_{\Phi}(x,y,\xi)=\mathrm{J}_{\varphi}(x)\mathrm{J}_{\varphi}(y)\mathrm{J}_{\varphi}^{-1}(\tfrac{x+y}{2})
.

Then, as
G\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{n})
, by Proposition
4.14
, we obtain

L_{\hbar}=(2\pi\hbar)^{n+1}\big(\tfrac{1}{\pi^{2}}Q\log\hbar^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(1)\big)

where, if it exists,

Q=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\bigg(\frac{1}{r}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}G(\omega,\omega,r\omega)g(\omega)^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega\bigg).

It remains to argue under the assumption (
4.44
) this limits is well-defined and (
4.53
) holds. We have as
r\to 0
,

\displaystyle G(w,w,rw)

\displaystyle=rR(\varphi(\omega))\mathrm{J}_{\varphi}(\omega)|D\varphi(\omega)^{-*}\omega|+\mathcal{O}(r^{2})

(4.63)

\displaystyle=rR(\hat{\varphi}(\omega))\mathrm{J}_{\hat{\varphi}}(\omega)+\mathcal{O}(r^{2})

where the map
\hat{\varphi}:\partial B\to\partial\Omega
is the
C^{\infty}
-diffeomorphism induced by
\varphi
on the boundary and the errors are controlled uniformly over
\omega\in\partial B
.
To obtain (
4.63
), note that for any
\omega\in\partial B
, we can decompose
T_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=T_{\omega}(\partial B)\oplus\mathbb{R}\omega
and, with
\hat{x}=\varphi(\omega)
,
T_{\hat{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=T_{\hat{x}}(\partial\Omega)\oplus\mathbb{R}\nu(\hat{x})
where
\nu(\hat{x})
is the (unit) normal to
\partial\Omega
at
\hat{x}
.
In this decomposition, since
\varphi^{-1}(\Omega)=B
the matrix of the differential
D\varphi
of the map
\varphi
has the following from

D\varphi=\begin{pmatrix}D\hat{\varphi}&0\\
\star&\alpha\end{pmatrix},\qquad\text{where}\quad\alpha(\omega)=\nu(\hat{x})\cdot D\varphi(\omega)\omega,\quad\hat{x}=\varphi(\omega).

(4.64)

In particular
\alpha>0
on
\partial B
and, by taking determinants, this implies that for
\omega\in\partial B
,

\mathrm{J}_{\varphi}(\omega)=\mathrm{J}_{\hat{\varphi}}(\omega)\alpha(\omega)

(4.65)

Moreover by definition of
\nu
, for any
\omega\in\partial B
and
v\in T_{\omega}(\partial B)=\omega^{\perp}
,

0=\nu(\varphi(\omega))\cdot D\varphi(\omega)u,

which shows that
D\varphi(\omega)^{*}\nu(\varphi(\omega))
is proportional to
\omega
.
According to (
4.64
), this implies that for
\omega\in\partial B
,

D\varphi(\omega)^{*}\nu(\varphi(\omega))=\alpha(\omega)\omega

and since
\big|\nu(\varphi(\omega))\big|=1
, we conclude that

1=\alpha(\omega)|D\varphi(\omega)^{-*}\omega|

Combined with (
4.65
), this proves formula (
4.63
).
Then, we deduce from this expansion that

Q=\int_{\partial B}R(\hat{\varphi}(\omega))f(\hat{\varphi}(\omega))^{2}\mathrm{J}_{\hat{\varphi}}(\omega)\mathrm{d}\omega=\int_{\partial\Omega}R(\hat{x})f(\hat{x})^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{x}

by a change of variable. In addition, we have

\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\bigg|\frac{G(w,w,rw)-rR(\hat{\varphi}(\omega))\mathrm{J}_{\hat{\varphi}}(\omega)}{r^{2}}\bigg|g(\omega)^{2}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}\omega<\infty.

which guarantees that the condition (
4.53
) holds.
This completes the proof. ∎

4.4
Partition of unity; Proof of Theorem
1
.

We now combine our previous results, with a decomposition using a partition of unity, to prove Theorem
1
.
Recall that
\Omega\Subset\mathcal{D}
is an open set with a smooth boundary and, according to Proposition
4.9
,
(2\pi\hbar)^{n-1}\big\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\mathcal{Q}(f)+\mathcal{O}(1)
as
\hbar\to 0
where
\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}
is the quadratic form

\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}:f\mapsto\frac{-1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n+1}}\int e^{i\frac{(x-y)\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}F(x,y,\xi)(f|_{\Omega}(x)-f|_{\Omega}(y))^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

acting on smooth functions.

Moreover, by Proposition
4.12
and (
4.13
), in case

\Omega
is contractible (
C^{\infty}
-diffeormorphic to
B_{n}
), one has

\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}(f)=\log\hbar^{-1}\mathcal{V}(f)+\mathcal{O}(1)

as
\hbar\to 0
where
\mathcal{V}
is the quadratic form

\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}:f\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{c}_{n-1}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\partial\Omega}|V(\hat{x})|^{\frac{n-1}{2}}f(\hat{x})^{2}\mathrm{d}\hat{x}

(4.66)

acting on smooth functions.

We claim that there is a smooth partition of unity
(\chi_{j})_{1\leq j\leq J}
such that

•

\chi_{j}|_{\Omega}=\chi_{j}|_{\Omega_{j}}
where
\Omega_{j}
is a contractible set, for
j\in[J]
.

•

(\chi_{i}+\chi_{j})|_{\Omega}=(\chi_{i}+\chi_{j})|_{\Omega_{i,j}}
where
\Omega_{i,j}
is a contractible set, for
i,j\in[J]
.

These sets are arbitrary smooth contractible sets. The condition
\chi|_{\Omega}=\chi|_{\Omega_{j}}
is equivalent to

\Omega\cap(\supp\chi)=\Omega_{j}\cap(\supp\chi),

so we can always choose a partition sufficiently fine so that these conditions hold. This partition is finite since
\Omega
is compact.

Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}(1)=\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}\big({\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}}\chi_{j}\big)

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{J}}\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}(\chi_{j}+\chi_{i})-{\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}}\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}(\chi_{j})

\displaystyle={\textstyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{J}}\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega_{ij}}(\chi_{j}+\chi_{i})-{\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}}\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega_{j}}(\chi_{j})

\displaystyle=\log\hbar^{-1}\Big({\textstyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{J}}\mathcal{V}_{\Omega_{ij}}(\chi_{j}+\chi_{i})-{\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}}\mathcal{V}_{\Omega_{j}}(\chi_{j})\Big)+\mathcal{O}(1)

Using that
\mathcal{V}_{\Omega_{ij}}(\chi_{j}+\chi_{i})=\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}(\chi_{j}+\chi_{i})
and
\mathcal{V}_{\Omega_{j}}(\chi_{j})=\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}(\chi_{j})
for
i,j\in[J]
, we conclude that

\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}(1)=\log\hbar^{-1}\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}\big({\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}}\chi_{j}\big)+\mathcal{O}(1).

Similarly, we have for any
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}(f)=\log\hbar^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{c}_{n-1}}{\pi^{2}}\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}(f)+\mathcal{O}(1)

as
\hbar\to 0
.

According to Proposition
2.5
, this shows
that for any open set
\Omega\Subset\mathcal{D}
with smooth
boundary and any
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

(2\pi\hbar)^{n-1}\big\|[\Pi,f|_{\Omega}]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=(2\pi\hbar)^{n-1}\big\|[\widetilde{\Pi},f|_{\Omega}]\big\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(1)=\log\hbar^{-1}\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}(f)+\mathcal{O}(1)\qquad\text{as $\hbar\to 0$}.

This completes the proof (with
\mu=0
and
V(x)<0
for
x\in\Omega
).

4.5
Central limit theorem; Proof of Theorem
2
.

The Gaussian fluctuations of the counting statistics, after rescaling,
are due to the determinantal structure of the free fermions point
process
\mathbf{X}
and the fact that for a non-trivial smooth set
\Omega
,

\operatorname{var}\mathbf{X}(\Omega)\to\infty
as
\hbar\to\infty
. In the
random matrix context, this observation is due to

[
13
]
and it has been extended to general
determinantal processes in
[
63
]
. By

[
63
, Thm 1]
, we deduce from
Theorem
1
that for any open set
\Omega\Subset\mathcal{D}
with smooth boundary and any
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
(in distribution) as
\hbar\to 0
,

\frac{\mathbf{X}(f|_{\Omega})-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}(f|_{\Omega})]}{\sqrt{-(2\pi\hbar)^{1-n}\log\hbar}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{0,\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}(f)}.

In particular, if
\Omega
has disjoint components
\{\Omega_{1},\cdots,\Omega_{k}\}
,

\mathcal{V}_{\Omega}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{V}_{\Omega_{j}}

according to (
4.66
),
then

\left(\frac{\mathbf{X}(\Omega_{1})-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}(\Omega_{1})]}{\sqrt{-(2\pi\hbar)^{1-n}\log\hbar}},\cdots,\frac{\mathbf{X}(\Omega_{k})-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}(\Omega_{k})]}{\sqrt{-(2\pi\hbar)^{1-n}\log\hbar}}\right)\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{0,\Sigma}

where

\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}\big(\mathcal{V}_{\Omega_{1}},\cdots,\mathcal{V}_{\Omega_{k}}\big).

Note that this argument cannot be directly applied to a collection of
sets
\Omega_{1},\cdots,\Omega_{k}\Subset\mathcal{D}
(open, with
smooth boundaries) with intersecting boundaries.
However, we can use the off-diagonal decay of the regularized kernel
\widetilde{\Pi}
and the estimates from Lemma
4.3
to show that the cross terms are negligible.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
\Omega\Subset\mathcal{D}
be open.
The kernel (
2.3
) satisfies for
(x,y)\in\Omega^{2}
,

|\widetilde{\Pi}(x,y)|^{2}\leq\frac{C\hbar^{1-n}}{(\hbar+|x-y|)^{n+1}}

Proof.

Let
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
be a smooth cutoff such that
\chi=1
on
\Omega
and
\chi=0
on
\Omega^{\prime}
where
\Omega^{\prime}
is a neighborhood of
\Omega
in
\mathcal{D}
.
We proceed as in Section
4.2.1
, we make a change of variable
\xi=r\omega
with
r>\underline{\mathrm{c}}
(cf. (
2.1
)),
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and we apply the stationary phase method to the integral (
2.3
) in the variables
(t,r)
with
(x,y,\omega,\lambda)
fixed.
The critical point is non-degenerate, given by (
4.18
) with
\sigma=0
, so we obtain

\chi(x)\widetilde{\Pi}(x,y)\chi(y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Psi_{1}(x,x-y,\omega,\lambda)}{\hbar}}b(x,y,\omega,\lambda)\mathds{1}\{\lambda\leq 0\}\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}\lambda

(4.67)

where
b\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3n+1})
is supported on
\big\{|\lambda|,|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell}\}
, and the phase
\Psi_{1}
vanishes on the diagonal

\{x=y\}
. This already implies the following saturated bound, locally uniformly in the bulk:

|\widetilde{\Pi}(x,y)|\leq C\hbar^{-n}.

Then, we can write

\Psi_{1}(x,u,\omega,\lambda)=u\cdot\zeta(x,u,\omega,\lambda)

where the map
\zeta:\mathbb{R}^{3n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is smooth on
\supp(b)

and make a change of coordinates
(\lambda,\omega)\to\zeta(x,u,\omega,\lambda)
in (
4.67
).
As in Section
4.2.2
(albeit using a simpler analysis), we claim that

\zeta(x,u,\omega,\lambda)=R(x,\lambda)\omega+\mathcal{O}(u),\qquad R=\sqrt{\lambda-V(x)}

and the error is smooth, so the matrix

\frac{\partial\zeta}{\partial\lambda\partial\omega}=R^{-1}\mathrm{I}_{n}+\mathcal{O}(u)

is non-degenerate since
R\geq\underline{\mathrm{c}}
on
\supp(b)
and
\underline{\ell}\ll\underline{\mathrm{c}}
. Hence, there is
g\in S^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3n})
, supported on
\big\{|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell}\}
, so that

\eqref{kern_red}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{(x-y)\cdot\zeta}{\hbar}}g(x,y,\zeta)\mathds{1}\{\lambda(x,y,\zeta)\leq 0\}\mathrm{d}\zeta

where the map
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{3n}\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth with on the diagonal
\{x=y\}
,

\lambda(x,x,\zeta)=|\zeta|^{2}+V(x).

By perturbation,
{\rm Hess}_{\zeta}\lambda(x,y,\zeta)
is positive-definite for
x\in\Omega^{\prime},|x-y|\leq\underline{\ell}
so that
\{\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\lambda(x,u,\zeta)\leq 0\}
is a strongly convex body.
Thus, by
[
31
, Corollary 7.7.15]
,
if
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\Lambda(x,y,u)
denotes the Fourier transform of
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathds{1}\{\lambda(x,y,\zeta)\leq 0\}
, then

\sup_{x,y\in\Omega^{\prime}}|\Lambda(x,y,u)|\leq\frac{C}{1+|u|^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}.

This is to be compared with the case of a ball (Lemma
4.16
).
This implies that

\chi(x)\widetilde{\Pi}(x,y)\chi(y)=\hbar^{-n}\Lambda(x,y,\cdot)*\widehat{g}(x,y,\cdot)|_{\tfrac{x-y}{\hbar}}

where
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\widehat{g}(x,y,u)
is a Schwartz function.
We conclude that

|\chi(x)\widetilde{\Pi}(x,y)\chi(y)|\leq C\hbar^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}|x-y|^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}.

which is the appropriate away from the diagonal.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

The bound from Lemma
4.17
corresponds to

|\widetilde{\Pi}(x,y)|\leq C\hbar^{-n}\big|\widehat{\mathds{1}}\{\cdot\leq 1\}(\frac{x-y}{\hbar})\big|
for
(x,y)\in\Omega^{2}
(inside the bulk).
This is consistent with the scaling limit of the regularized kernel
\widetilde{\Pi}
. Namely, we showed in
[
19
]
that for
x\in\mathcal{D}
,

\hbar^{n}\widetilde{\Pi}(x+\hbar u,x+\hbar v)\to K(u-v)
where
K=C_{n}\widehat{\mathds{1}}\{\cdot\leq c_{n}\}
for some constants
C_{n},c_{n}
depending only on the dimension
n
.

Given two open sets
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}\Subset\mathcal{D}
with smooth boundary such that
|\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2}|=0
, Lemmas
4.17
and
4.3
imply that as
\hbar\to\infty
,

\big|\tr([\widetilde{\Pi},\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}][\widetilde{\Pi},\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}])\big|\leq C\hbar^{1-n}\int\frac{|(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}(y))(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(x)-\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}(y))|}{(\hbar+|x-y|)^{n+1}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=o\big(\log(\hbar^{-1})\hbar^{1-n}\big).

Then, expanding the square, under the assumptions of Theorem
2
, it holds for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\frac{[\widetilde{\Pi},{\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\alpha_{k}\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{k}}}]\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n-1}(\log\hbar^{-1})}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{\alpha_{k}[\widetilde{\Pi},\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{k}}]\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n-1}(\log\hbar^{-1})}+o(1)\qquad\text{as $\hbar\to 0$}.

Replacing
\widetilde{\Pi}
by the projector
\Pi
using Proposition
2.5
, we conclude by Theorem
1
that as
\hbar\to 0
,

\frac{\|[\Pi,{\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\alpha_{k}\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{k}}}]\|^{2}_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n-1}(\log\hbar^{-1})}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\alpha_{k}\mathcal{V}_{\Omega_{k}}(1)+o(1).

Again, applying Soshnikov’s result
[
63
, Thm 1]
, by the Cramér–Wold argument, this completes the proof of Theorem
2
.

One has the following more precise covariance estimate in the case of
a relatively non-singular intersection.

Remark 4.19
.

If
H^{\beta}(\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2})<+\infty
for
some
\beta<n-1
, then the last covariance bound
improves to

{\rm Cov}(\mathbf{X}(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{1}}),\mathbf{X}(\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{2}}))=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})

using the second part of Lemma
4.3
. At this scale, we
cannot use the regularised projector from
Proposition
2.5
to compute the limit
of the covariance.
This difficulty already appears in the variance estimates in
[
19
]
.

5
Trace norm of commutators & entropy estimates

5.1
Proof of Theorem
4
: Multi-scale argument

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
4
, that is, to
show that for any open set
\Omega\Subset\mathcal{D}
with a smooth boundary

\big\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}\big(\hbar^{1-n}|\log\hbar|^{2}\big).

(5.1)

We begin by a simple truncation step.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\vartheta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be any smooth function which equal to
1
on
[-\underline{\mathrm{c}}/2,\underline{\mathrm{c}}/2]
.
Let
\Gamma_{\pm}=\vartheta\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{\pm}}(H)
and
\Theta_{\pm}=\vartheta 1_{\mathbb{R}_{\pm}}(\mathrm{w})
.
It holds

\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}\leq\|\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{+}\Gamma_{+}\Theta_{-}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}+\|\Gamma_{+}\Theta_{+}\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{-}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}+\|\Gamma_{+}\Theta_{-}\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{+}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}+\|\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{-}\Gamma_{+}\Theta_{+}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}+\mathcal{O}\big(\hbar^{1-n}\big).

Proof.

First observe that since the operator
H
and the map
\mathrm{w}
are bounded from below

\begin{cases}\Pi=\Gamma_{-}+f_{1}(H)\\
1-\Pi=\Gamma_{+}+1-f_{2}(H)\end{cases}\qquad\qquad\begin{cases}\mathds{1}_{\Omega}=\Theta_{-}+f_{1}(\mathrm{w})\\
1-\Pi=\Theta_{+}+1-f_{2}(\mathrm{w})\end{cases}

(5.2)

where
f_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
are smooth functions, supported in
(-\infty,\underline{\mathrm{c}}]
with
f_{1}\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=0
and
(1-f_{2})\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{-}}=0
.
Second, observe that

\displaystyle[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}-\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}

\displaystyle=\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}+(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}-(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}-\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}.

Then, using that
\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}=0
, we can change the
first and fourth terms as follows:

[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]=-\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega}+(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}-(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}+\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}.

(5.3)

Every term on the RHS of (
5.3
) can be handled in the same way, so we focus on the last one.
Using (
5.2
), since
f_{1}(H)(1-\Pi)=0
and
\Gamma_{-}(1-f_{2}(H))=0
, one has

\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}=\Gamma_{-}\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Gamma_{+}\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}+[f_{1}(H),\mathds{1}_{\Omega}](1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}+\Gamma_{-}[\mathds{1}_{\Omega},1-f_{2}(H)]\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}

According to Proposition
2.6
, both
\|[f_{j}(H),\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, so

\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}=\Gamma_{-}\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Gamma_{+}\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}(\hbar^{1-n}).

Similarly,

\Gamma_{-}\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Gamma_{+}\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}=\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{-}\Gamma_{+}\Theta_{+}+\Gamma_{-}[f_{1}(\mathrm{w}),\Gamma_{+}]\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}+\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{-}[\Gamma_{+},1-f_{2}(\mathrm{w})].

Observe that according to Lemma
2.9
and (
1.10
),

\displaystyle[f_{1}(\mathrm{w}),\Gamma_{\pm}]=[f_{1}(\mathrm{w}),f_{j}(H)]-[f_{1}(\mathrm{w}),\Pi]=\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}(\hbar^{1-n}).

Thus, we conclude that

\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}(1-\Pi)\mathds{1}_{\Omega^{c}}=\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{-}\Gamma_{+}\Theta_{+}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}(\hbar^{1-n}).\qed

Again, every term in the bound from Lemma
5.1
can be handled in the same way.
We proceed using a dyadic argument to
isolate the contributions from different scales
that we will estimate based on Proposition
3.1
.
To setup the dyadic decompositions, on top of the notations from Section
2.1
, we use the following convention throughout this section.

Notations 5.1
.

•

Let
\mathrm{w}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function such that
\Omega=\{\mathrm{w}<0\}\Subset\mathcal{D}
and
\partial_{x}\mathrm{w}(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in\partial\Omega=\{\mathrm{w}=0\}
.
In particular, we assume that
\partial_{x}\mathrm{w}\neq 0
on
\{|\mathrm{w}|\leq\underline{\mathrm{c}}\}
by choosing the constant
\underline{\mathrm{c}}
sufficiently small.

•

Let
\vartheta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
supported in
[-\underline{\mathrm{c}},\underline{\mathrm{c}}]
and equal to
1
on
[-\underline{\mathrm{c}}/2,\underline{\mathrm{c}}/2]
.
Let
\chi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
supported in
[-1,1]
and equal to 1 on
[-\underline{\mathrm{c}},\underline{\mathrm{c}}]
.

•

Let
L:=\lfloor\log_{2}(\hbar)\rfloor
and let
\eta_{k}:=\hbar 2^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{<L}
.

•

Let
\chi_{0}:=\chi(\hbar^{-1}\cdot)
and

\chi_{k}:x\mapsto\chi_{0}(2^{-k}x)-\chi_{0}(2^{1-k}x),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}_{<L}.

We also let
\chi_{L}:x\mapsto\chi(x)-\chi_{0}(2^{1-L}x)
.
We use a similar notation for
(\vartheta_{k})_{k=0}^{L}
.

•

We use the shorthand notation,
\chi_{k}^{\pm}=\chi_{k}\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{\pm}}

for
k\in[0,L]
; similarly for
(\vartheta_{k}^{\pm})_{k=0}^{L}
.

•

We let
\psi_{k}^{\pm}:=\vartheta\cdot(\chi_{k}^{\pm}*\rho_{\hbar})
for
k\in[1,L]
, as in the
Notation of Section
2.1
.
In particular,
\chi_{k}^{\pm},\psi_{k}^{\pm}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
are in
S^{\eta_{k}}
for
k\in[1,L]
.

•

For
k\in[1,L]
, let
\breve{\vartheta}_{k}^{\pm}
be in
S^{\eta_{k}}
such that

\chi_{k}^{\pm}=\chi_{k}^{\pm}\breve{\chi}_{k}^{\pm},\qquad\chi_{k}^{\mp}\breve{\chi}_{m}^{\pm}=0,\qquad\text{for~$k,m\in[1,L]$}.

(5.4)

This yields a dyadic decomposition of the operators appearing in Lemma
5.1
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Using the notation
5.1
, we have

\Theta_{\pm}=\sum_{k=0}^{L}\vartheta_{k}(\mathrm{w})

and

\Gamma_{\pm}=\sum_{k=1}^{L}\widetilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{k}(H)+\Upsilon_{\pm}
where the errors satisfy

\|\Upsilon_{\pm}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})\qquad\qquad\|\Upsilon_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq 1.

Proof.

The decomposition of
\Theta_{\pm}
is obvious, so we focus on the decomposition of
\Gamma_{+}
(
\Gamma_{-}
is handled similarly).
By construction
\sum_{k=0}^{L}\chi_{k}^{+}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
on
[-\underline{\mathrm{c}},\underline{\mathrm{c}}]
. So, since
\rho
is a Schwartz mollifier, we claim that for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
\varkappa_{m}:\mathbb{R}\to[-1,1]
, smooth with compact support so that

\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{L}\chi_{k}^{+}*\rho_{\hbar}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}+\varkappa_{m}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{m})\qquad\text{with a smooth error on $[-\underline{\mathrm{c}},\underline{\mathrm{c}}]$}.

By Lemma
2.7
, this implies that
\sum_{k=1}^{L}\widetilde{\chi}^{\pm}_{k}(H)=\Gamma_{+}+\Upsilon_{+}
where the operator
\Upsilon_{+}
satisfies

\|\Upsilon_{\pm}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-n})\qquad\qquad\|\Upsilon_{\pm}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq 1.

∎

We are now ready to proceed to obtain the estimate (
5.1
).

Proof of Theorem
4
.

According to Lemma
5.1
, it suffices to show that

\|\Gamma_{+}\Theta_{+}\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{-}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}\leq\mathrm{C}\hbar^{1-n}L^{2}
;
since the other terms are handled similarly.
By Lemma
5.2
,

\Gamma_{+}\Theta_{+}\Gamma_{-}\Theta_{-}=\sum_{i,k\geq 1}\sum_{j,m\geq 0}\widetilde{\chi}^{+}_{i}(H)\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}^{-}_{k}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}(\hbar^{1-n})

where the indices range up to
L
.
The idea is to split this sum in two parts:

\mathrm{I}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,k\geq 1;j,m\geq 0\\
i+j\leq k+m\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\chi}^{+}_{i}(H)\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}^{-}_{k}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{II}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,k\geq 1;j,m\geq 0\\
i+j>k+m\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\chi}^{+}_{i}(H)\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}^{-}_{k}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})

Both sums will be handled in a similar way, so we focus on
\mathrm{I}
for now. Using (
5.4
), one has

\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{+}(H)\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})=\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{+}(H)\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\big[\big[\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H),\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\big],\breve{\vartheta}_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\big].

Then, we can bound

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{+}(H)\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}\leq\|\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{+}(H)\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}\begin{cases}\|\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}&\text{if }\eta_{k}\eta_{m}\leq\hbar\\
\big\|\big[\big[\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H),\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\big],\breve{\vartheta}_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}&\text{if }\eta_{k}\eta_{m}>\hbar\end{cases}.

According to Proposition
3.1
, there exists a constant
C>0
so that for any
k\in[1,L]
and
m\in[0,L]
,

\begin{cases}\big\|\big[\big[\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{\pm}(H),\vartheta_{m}^{\pm}(\mathrm{w})\big],\breve{\vartheta}_{m}^{\pm}(\mathrm{w})\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\hbar^{3-n}\eta_{k}^{-2}\eta_{m}^{-2}=C\hbar^{-1-n}2^{-2(k+m)}&\text{if }\eta_{k}\eta_{m}>\hbar,\\
\|\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{\pm}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{\pm}(\mathrm{w})\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\hbar^{-n}\eta_{k}\|\vartheta_{m}^{\pm}(\mathrm{w})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\hbar^{-n}\eta_{k}\eta_{m}=C\hbar^{2-n}2^{k+m}.\end{cases}

Here we used that
\|\vartheta_{m}^{\pm}(\mathrm{w})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\eta_{m}
for
m\in[0,L]
.
Altogether, this implies that

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{I}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,k\geq 1;j,m\geq 0\\
i+j\leq k+m\end{subarray}}\|\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{+}(H)\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\Bigg(\hbar^{2-n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\leq k+m\\
\hbar 2^{k+m}\leq 1\end{subarray}}2^{\frac{i+j+k+m}{2}}+\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}-n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\leq k+m\\
1\leq\hbar 2^{k+m}\end{subarray}}2^{\frac{i+j}{2}-(k+m)}\Bigg).

Since all indices range up to
L
, these sums are controlled by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\leq k+m\\
\hbar 2^{k+m}\leq 1\end{subarray}}2^{\frac{i+j+k+m}{2}}\leq L^{2}\sum_{\hbar 2^{m}\leq 1}2^{m}\leq 2\hbar^{-1}L^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\leq k+m\\
1\leq\hbar 2^{k+m}\end{subarray}}2^{\frac{i+j}{2}-(k+m)}\leq L^{2}\sum_{\hbar 2^{m}\geq 1}2^{-\frac{m}{2}}\leq 2\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}.

Hence, we conclude that

\|\mathrm{I}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}\big(L^{2}\hbar^{1-n}\big).

(5.5)

We have a similar control for
\mathrm{II}
, which we expect by symmetry.
Let us first record that

\sum_{i,k\geq 1}\sum_{j,m\geq 0}\big\|\big[\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{+}(H),\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}\big\|\big[\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H),\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}=\bigg(\sum_{k\geq 1,m\geq 0}\big\|\big[\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{+}(H),\vartheta_{m}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}\bigg)^{2}

and, as above (by Proposition
3.1
),

\sum_{k\geq 1,m\geq 0}\big\|\big[\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{+}(H),\vartheta_{m}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\big]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}\leq C\bigg(\hbar^{1-\frac{n}{2}}\sum_{\hbar 2^{k+m}\leq 1}2^{\frac{k+m}{2}}+\hbar^{-\frac{n}{2}}\sum_{\hbar 2^{k+m}\geq 1}2^{-\frac{k+m}{2}}\bigg)=\mathcal{O}\big(L\hbar^{\frac{1-n}{2}}\big).

This estimate shows that

\mathrm{II}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,k\geq 1;j,m\geq 0\\
i+j>k+m\end{subarray}}\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{+}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}\big(L^{2}\hbar^{1-n}\big).

Now, using that

\vartheta_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{+}(H)\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H)=\big[\breve{\vartheta}_{j}^{+}(\mathrm{w}),\big[\vartheta_{j}^{-}(\mathrm{w}),\widetilde{\chi}_{i}^{-}(H)\big]\big]\vartheta_{m}^{-}(\mathrm{w})\widetilde{\chi}_{k}^{-}(H)

we can proceed exactly as above to show that

\|\mathrm{II}\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}\big(L^{2}\hbar^{1-n}\big).

This completes the proof.
∎

5.2
Bounds for the entanglement entropy: Proof of Theorem
3

The estimates of Theorem
3
for the (entanglement) entropy follow by interpolation from the bounds for the Hilbert-Schmidt and trace norm of the commutator
[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]
of Theorems
1
and
4
.
Our goal is to prove the following general inequalities.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a determinantal process on a Polish space
\mathcal{X}

associated with a (self-adjoint) operator
0<\Pi\leq 1

locally trace-class. Then, for any open set
\Omega\Subset\mathcal{X}
with smooth boundary,

2\big\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{S}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{X})\leq 4\big\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}\log\bigg(\frac{\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}}{\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}}\bigg).

Since we establish that
\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}\simeq\mathrm{c}\hbar^{1-n}\log(\hbar^{-1})
for some constant
\mathrm{c}>0
if

\Omega\Subset\mathcal{D}
is an open set with smooth boundary
and
\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}\leq C\log(\hbar^{-1})\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}
as
\hbar\to 0
, Theorem
3
follows directly from Lemma
5.3
. We now turn to its proof.

Let
s:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, given by
s:\lambda\mapsto-\lambda\log(\lambda)-(1-\lambda)\log(1-\lambda)
. Recall that for a
general determinantal point process
\mathbf{X}
, defined on a Polish space

\mathcal{S}
and associated with a locally trace-class operator

0<\Pi\leq 1
, the (entanglement) entropy of any open set
\Omega\Subset\mathcal{X}
with smooth boundary is

\mathcal{S}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{X})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}s(\lambda_{n}),

(5.6)

where
\{\lambda_{n}\}
denote the non-trivial eigenvalues of operator
\Pi|_{\Omega}=\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}
–
\{\lambda_{n}\}
is a countable sequence in
(0,1]
by the spectral theorem for locally compact operators.

The main step of the proof is to describe the relationship between the
spectra of
\Pi\mathds{1}_{\Omega}\Pi
and the square-commutator
-[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]^{2}
. This follows from a general result.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
P,Q
be two self-adjoint projections on a separable Hilbert space.
The non-negative operators
PQP
,
-[P,Q]^{2}

commute.
The spectral data of
-[P,Q]^{2}
, on the orthogonal of its kernel, consists exactly of the eigenpairs

(\lambda(1-\lambda),u)
and
(\lambda(1-\lambda),[P,Q]u)
,
where
(\lambda,u)
is an eigenpair of

PQP
with
\lambda>0
.

Thus, given
f:[0,\frac{1}{4}]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
continuous with
f(0)=0
and

g:\lambda\in[0,1]\mapsto f(\lambda(1-\lambda))
, one has

\tr f(-[P,Q]^{2})=2\tr g(PQP).

(5.7)

Proof.

Using that
P^{2}=P
and
Q^{2}=Q
, we simply compute

P[P,Q]^{2}=[P,Q]^{2}P=PQPQP-PQP=PQP(PQP-1).

(5.8)

So
(P,-[P,Q]^{2})
commute.
Then, using that
(P,-[P,Q]^{2})
and
(Q,-[P,Q]^{2})
commute, we also have

[PQP,[P,Q]^{2}]=PQ[P,Q]^{2}P-P[P,Q]^{2}QP=P[Q,[P,Q]^{2}]P=0.

Thus
(PQP,-[P,Q]^{2})
also commute as claimed.

Since
(P,-[P,Q]^{2})
commute, the spectral data of
-[P,Q]^{2}
can be decomposed into the spectral data of
-P[P,Q]^{2}P
and
-(1-P)[P,Q]^{2}(1-P)
.
Then, (
5.8
) shows that any eigenpair
(\lambda,u)
of
PQP
with
\lambda>0
gives an eigenpair
(\lambda(1-\lambda),u)
of
-P[P,Q]^{2}P
.
This means that for
\sigma>0
, the eigenspace of
-P[P,Q]^{2}P
associated with
\sigma
is

F_{\sigma}=\ker(PQP-\lambda)
if
\sigma=\lambda(1-\lambda)
.
By the spectral theorem, this gives the complete spectral data of
-P[P,Q]^{2}P
, on the orthogonal of its kernel.

Now, let
\sigma>0
be an eigenvalue of
-(1-P)[P,Q]^{2}(1-P)
and let
E_{\sigma}
the associated eigenspace and

T:=[P,Q]
.
Since
E_{\sigma}\subset\ker(P)
, one has for
u\in E_{\sigma}
,

Tu=PQu,\qquad-[P,Q]^{2}u=\sigma u\qquad\text{and}\qquad-P[P,Q]^{2}P(Tu)=-PQ[P,Q]^{2}u=\sigma PQu=\sigma Tu

using the commutation relations.
This shows that
(\sigma,Tu)
is an eigenpair of
-P[P,Q]^{2}P
.
Moreover,
T^{2}=-\sigma\mathrm{I}
on
E_{\sigma}
, so that
T:E_{\sigma}\to F_{\sigma}
is 1-1.

In conclusion,
\ker(-[P,Q]^{2}-\sigma)=\operatorname{span}\{u,Tu:u\in\ker(PQP-\lambda)\}
if
\sigma=\lambda(1-\lambda)
with
\lambda>0
so that choosing an orthonormal basis
\{(\lambda_{k},u_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of eigenfunction of
PQP
, by the spectral theorem (
PQP\geq 0
), for any
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, continuous with
f(0)=0
, one has

\tr f(-[P,Q]^{2})=2\sum_{\lambda_{k}>0}f(\lambda_{k}(1-\lambda_{k}))=2\tr g(PQP)

where
g(\lambda)=f(\lambda(1-\lambda))
.
Note that since
PQP\leq 1
, one can consider only test functions
f
defined on
[0,\tfrac{1}{4}]
, both sides of (
5.7
) are non-negative and possibly infinite.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Considering Widom’s conjecture, in light of Lemma
5.4
, the spectral asymptotics of Conjecture
1
are equivalent to, given
f:[0,\frac{1}{4}]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
continuous with
f(0)=0
,

\frac{\tr f(-[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]^{2})}{{(2\pi\hbar)^{1-n}}\log\hbar^{-1}}\to 8C_{\Omega}\int_{[0,\tfrac{1}{4}]}\frac{f(\sigma)}{\sigma\sqrt{1-4\sigma}}\mathrm{d}\sigma.

We now turn to show the inequalities for the entropy.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Considering (
5.6
), the map
s=f(\lambda(1-\lambda))
where
f:[0,\frac{1}{4}]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is given by

f:\sigma\mapsto-\tfrac{1-\sqrt{1-4\sigma}}{2}\log(\tfrac{1-\sqrt{1-4\sigma}}{2})-\tfrac{1+\sqrt{1-4\sigma}}{2}\log(\tfrac{1+\sqrt{1-4\sigma}}{2}).

This function is continuous with
f(0)=0
(one has
f(\sigma)\sim-\sigma\log(\sigma)
as
\sigma\to 0
)
so that, according to Lemma
5.4
, we can rewrite

\mathcal{S}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{X})=\tr f(-[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]^{2}).

The proof is based on the basic inequalities, for
\sigma\in[0,\frac{1}{4}]
,

\mathrm{c}\sigma\leq f(\sigma)\leq-2\sigma\log\sigma.

(5.9)

Observe that these function are all smooth, increasing, concave on
(0,\frac{1}{4})
and take the values
0
, respectively
\log 2
at the endpoints; so the inequality (
5.9
) follows from the fact that

f^{\prime}(\sigma)\sim\log(1/\sigma)
as
\sigma\to 0
.

Thus, by monotonicity, with
g:\sigma\mapsto-2\sigma\log\sigma
, we obtain

\mathrm{c}\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{S}_{\Omega}\leq\tr g(-[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]^{2}).

We now relate the upper-bound to the trace-norm of the commutator
[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]
. We can assume that
\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}<\infty
, otherwise there is nothing to prove, in which case
\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sigma_{n}<\infty

where
\{\sigma_{n}\}
denotes the non-zero eigenvalues of
-[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]^{2}
. Then, by convexity of
\sigma\mapsto\log(\sigma^{-1/2})
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and Jensen’s inequality,

{\textstyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{n}\log(\sigma_{n}^{-1/2})\leq{\textstyle\big(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{n}\big)\log\bigg(\frac{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sigma_{n}^{1/2}}{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sigma_{n}}\bigg)

or equivalently,

\tfrac{1}{4}\tr g(-[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]^{2})\leq\big\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\big\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}\log\bigg(\frac{\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{1}}}{\|[\Pi,\mathds{1}_{\Omega}]\|_{\mathrm{J}^{2}}^{2}}\bigg).

This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Stationary phase with mild amplitudes

We first recall a standard version of the stationary phase method where the amplitude is controlled in
\mathscr{C}^{k}
, independently of semiclassical parameter
\hbar
, and the integral depends on a parameter
z\in\mho
.
We refer for instance to theorem 7.7.5 in
[
31
]
or to
our previous work
[
19
, Proposition A.15]
.

Proposition A.1

(Stationary phase lemma)
.

For
d,q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mho\Subset\mathbb{R}^{q}
be open.
Let
\Phi:\Omega\times\mho\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function
2
2

2

In fact, it is not necessary to assume that the phase
\Phi
and the symbol
a
are smooth with respect to the parameter
z\in\mho
; continuity suffices.
such that
\partial_{x}\Phi(x,z)=0
has a unique solution
(x_{z},z)\in\Omega\times\mho
with
\Phi(x_{z},z)=0
and the Hessian
\nabla^{2}_{x}\Phi(x_{z},z)
is non-degenerate for
z\in\mho
.
Let
a\in S^{1}(\Omega\times\mho)
be classical symbol.
Then, there exists another classical symbol
b\in S^{1}(\mho)
so that

\int_{\Omega}e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=(2\pi\hbar)^{\frac{d}{2}}b(z,\hbar)

and the principal part of
b
is given by
b_{0}(z)=a_{0}(x_{z},z)/\sqrt{\det\nabla^{2}_{x}\Phi(x_{z},z)}
for
z\in A
.

On the other hand, if
x\mapsto\Phi(x,z)
has no critical points in
\Omega
, then

\int_{\Omega}e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty})

where the error term is controlled in
S^{1}(\mho)
.

The goal of this section is to generalize the above expansion when the symbol
a\in S^{\delta}
is sufficiently regular (
\delta\geq\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
)
with an explicit control of the error.
We begin by the simple case where the phase
\Phi
has no critical point within the support of the symbol
a
.

Lemma A.2
.

For
d,q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mho\Subset\mathbb{R}^{q}
be open.
Let
\Phi:\Omega\times\mho\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function such that
\partial_{x}\Phi(x,z)=0
has a unique solution
(x_{z},z)\in\Omega\times\mho
and the Hessian
\nabla^{2}_{x}\Phi(x_{z},z)
is non-degenerate for all
z\in\mho
.
Let
\delta\geq\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
a\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\Omega\times\mho)
with
a\in S^{\delta}_{x}
and suppose that for
z\in\mho
,
a(x,z)=0
for

x\in B(x_{z},\epsilon)
for some
\epsilon\geq\delta
. Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{z\in\mho}\bigg|\hbar^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\frac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\leq C_{k}\left(\frac{\hbar}{\delta\epsilon}\right)^{k}.

In particular, if
a\in S^{\delta}(\Omega\times\mho)
with
\delta\geq\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\epsilon\delta\gg\hbar
, then as
\hbar\to 0

\int e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{C}^{\infty}}(\hbar^{\infty}).

(A.1)

Proof.

To ease notation, we treat the case without parameter:
\mho=\emptyset
,
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function with a unique non-degenerate critical point
x_{\emptyset}\in\Omega
and
a\in S^{\delta}(\Omega)
with
a=0
on

B(x_{\emptyset},\epsilon)
with
\epsilon\geq\delta\geq\hbar^{1/2}
.
By assumptions,
x\mapsto\frac{a(x)}{|\nabla\Phi(x)|^{2}}
is a smooth function, so that integrating by parts, we have

\int e^{i\frac{\Phi(x)}{\hbar}}a(x)\mathrm{d}x=i\hbar\int e^{i\frac{\Phi(x)}{\hbar}}\frac{a(x)\Psi(x)+\nabla a(x)\cdot\nabla\Phi(x)}{|\nabla\Phi(x)|^{2}}\mathrm{d}x

where
\Psi:x\mapsto\Delta\Phi(x)+|\nabla\Phi(x)|^{2}\nabla\Phi(x)\cdot\nabla|\nabla\Phi(x)|^{-2}
is also smooth on
\Omega
.
We can perform this operation
k
times, and we obtain differential operators
L^{j;k}
of degree
\leq j
(taking values into symmetric
j
-tensors) whose coefficients are smooth functions (depending on
\Phi
) so that

\int e^{i\frac{\Phi(x)}{\hbar}}a_{\hbar}(x)\mathrm{d}x=(i\hbar)^{k}\int e^{i\frac{\Phi(x)}{\hbar}}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\frac{\nabla\Phi(x)^{\otimes j}\cdot L^{j;k}a(x)}{|\nabla\Phi(x)|^{2k}}\mathrm{d}x.

(A.2)

Since
x_{\emptyset}
is the unique non-degenerate critical point of

\Phi
,
|\nabla\Phi(x)|\asymp|x-x_{\emptyset}|
for
x\in\Omega
, so
we have the following estimates, for every
0\leq j\leq k
:

\frac{|\nabla\Phi(x)^{\otimes j}\cdot L^{j;k}a(x)|}{|\nabla\Phi(x)|^{2k}}\leq C_{k}\delta^{-j}|x-x_{\emptyset}|^{-2k+j},\qquad x\in\Omega.

Then, using that
a=0
on

B(x_{\emptyset},\epsilon)
with
\epsilon\geq\delta
, we obtain for every
k\geq d
and for every
0\leq j\leq k
,

\int\frac{|\nabla\Phi(x)^{\otimes j}\cdot L^{j;k}a(x)|}{|\nabla\Phi(x)|^{2k}}\mathrm{d}x\leq C_{k}\delta^{-j}\epsilon^{-2k+j+d}\leq C_{k}\delta^{-k}\epsilon^{-k+d}.

We conclude that for every
k\geq d
,

\bigg|\hbar^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\Omega}e^{i\frac{\Phi(x)}{\hbar}}a(x)\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\leq C_{k}\hbar^{k-d/2}\delta^{-k}\epsilon^{-k+d}=C_{k}\left(\frac{\hbar}{\delta\epsilon}\right)^{k-d}\left(\frac{\hbar^{1/2}}{\delta}\right)^{d}

which proves the claim in case
\mho=\emptyset
using the condition
\delta\geq\hbar^{1/2}
.
The argument remains the same if
(\Phi,a)
depend continuously on a parameter
z\in\mho
and the estimates are uniform provided that
\mho
is relatively compact.
In particular, if
\epsilon\delta\gg\hbar
, we obtain uniformly for
z\in\mho
,

\int e^{i\frac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty}).

If we apply
\partial_{z}^{\alpha}
to the LHS, we obtain a similar integral with another symbol
a_{\alpha}\in S^{\delta}
times
\hbar^{-|\alpha|}
, so this proves (
A.1
).
∎

Proposition
A.2
already implies that such oscillatory integrals are uniformly bounded.

Corollary A.3
.

Let
\Phi
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
A.2
.
Then, for any
a\in S^{\delta}(\Omega\times\mho)
with
\delta\geq\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
as
\hbar\to 0
,

\hbar^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\Omega}e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}_{S^{\delta}}(1).

Proof.

By a change of variable, we can assume without loss of generality that
x_{z}=0
for
z\in\mho
and
\delta=\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a cutoff with
\chi=1
on the unit ball and we split

\hbar^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\Omega}e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=\hbar^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\Omega}e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\chi(x/\delta)\mathrm{d}x+\hbar^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\Omega}e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}b(x,z)\mathrm{d}x

where
b:(x,z)\mapsto a(x,z)(1-\chi(\delta^{-1}x))
is also in
S^{\delta}
. Hence, by
Lemma
A.2
, the second integral is
\mathcal{O}(1)
, while the first integral is also
\mathcal{O}(1)
by a direct volume estimate.
∎

Then, we can improve this a-priori estimate by giving the asymptotic expansion of such oscillatory integrals using the stationary phase method with precise estimates on the error term.

Proposition A.4
.

For
d,q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mho\Subset\mathbb{R}^{q}
be open.
Let
\Phi:\Omega\times\mho\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function such that
\partial_{x}\Phi(x,z)=0
has a unique solution
(x_{z},z)\in\Omega\times\mho
with
\Phi(x_{z},z)=0
and the Hessian
\nabla^{2}_{x}\Phi(x_{z},z)
is non-degenerate for
z\in\mho
.
Let
a\in S^{\delta}(\Omega\times\mho)
with
\delta\geq\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Then for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, as
\hbar\to 0
,

(2\pi i\hbar)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{0\leq j<\ell}\hbar^{j}L^{j}_{x}a(x_{z},z)+\mathcal{O}_{S^{\delta}}\big((\hbar\delta^{-2})^{\ell}\big)

(A.3)

where, for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
L^{j}_{x}
is a differential operator
(
acting on
x)
of degree
2j
whose coefficients depend only
on the phase
\Phi
.
In addition, if
\supp(a)\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, then for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\epsilon\in[\delta,1]
, as
\hbar\to 0
,

(2\pi i\hbar)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}_{S^{\delta}}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{\hbar}{\delta\epsilon}\bigg)^{\ell}\bigg)\qquad\text{uniformly on }\big\{z\in\mho:\dist\big((x_{z},z),\mathcal{A}\big)\geq\epsilon\big\}.

(A.4)

Proof.

To compute the integral (
A.3
), we apply Morse lemma (with parameters) to the phase
\Phi
, see
[
31
, p502]
.
By Lemma
A.2
applied with a fixed
\epsilon
, we can assume that
a
is supported on an arbitrary small neighborhood
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d\times q}
of the critical point
(x_{z},z)
, up to a negligible error (in the sense of (
A.1
)).
Then, by a
C^{\infty}
diffeormorphism
\varphi
, there is coordinate system such that the critical points are
(0,z)
and the phase

\Phi(x,z)=\frac{x\cdot Hx}{2},\qquad H=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d-\alpha}&0\\
0&-I_{\alpha}\end{pmatrix},

where
\alpha\in[0,d]
is the Morse index, that is, the number of negative eigenvalues of the non-degenerate matrix matrix
\nabla^{2}_{x}\Phi(x_{z},z)
– by continuity
\alpha
is constant (independent of
z\in\mho
and so is the phase
\Phi
in this new coordinate system).
Moreover, the symbol
b=a(\varphi)\in S^{\delta}
with the same
\delta
. Consequently, it suffices to show that the integral

R_{0}:z\mapsto(2\pi i\hbar)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\frac{xHx}{2\hbar}}b(x,z)\mathrm{d}x

has an expansion of the type (
A.3
) as
\hbar\to 0
.
First, by Corollary
A.3
, since the phase is independent of
z
, by differentiating under the integral,
R_{0}\in S^{\delta}(\mho)
and it satisfies (
A.4
), by Lemma
A.2
.

Let
L:=i(\nabla_{x}\cdot H\nabla_{x})
and
b_{1}:=\delta^{2}Lb
.
Observe that if we integrate by parts twice, for any
\lambda>0
,

-\partial_{\lambda}\left[\lambda^{\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\lambda\tfrac{xHx}{2\hbar}}b(x,z)\mathrm{d}x\right]=\hbar\lambda^{\frac{d}{2}-2}\int e^{i\lambda\tfrac{xHx}{2\hbar}}Lb(x,z)\mathrm{d}x.

(A.5)

Moreover, by the standard stationary phase (Proposition
A.1
– for a fixed
\hbar
with
b
independent of
\lambda
):

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to+\infty}\left(\frac{\lambda}{2\pi i\hbar}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\lambda\tfrac{xHx}{2\hbar}}b(x,z)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=i^{-\alpha}b(0,z)

(A.6)

\displaystyle=i^{-\alpha}|\det\nabla^{2}_{x}\Phi(x_{z},z)|^{-1/2}a(x_{z},z)

going back to the original coordinate system.
We have
b_{1}\in S^{\delta}
, so by Corollary
A.3
,

\sup_{z\in\mho,\lambda\geq 1}\bigg|\hbar^{-\frac{d}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\lambda\tfrac{xHx}{2\hbar}}b_{1}(x,z)\mathrm{d}x\bigg|=O(1)

and

R_{1}(z):=(2\pi i\hbar)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{1}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{d}{2}-2}\bigg\{\int e^{i\lambda\tfrac{xHx}{2\hbar}}b_{1}(x,z)\mathrm{d}x\bigg\}\mathrm{d}\lambda

is also in
S^{\delta}
(just like the symbol
b_{1}
by differentiating under the integral).
Thus, integrating both sides of (
A.5
) for
\lambda\in[1,\infty)
, by (
A.6
), we obtain

R_{0}(z)=i^{-\alpha}b(0,z)+\hbar\delta^{-2}R_{1}(z).

This proves the claim for
\ell=1
with
L_{0}=i^{-\alpha}
(in this coordinate system).
The general expansion follows by induction: for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, let
b_{\ell}:=\delta^{2\ell}L^{\ell}b
and

R_{\ell}(z):=(2\pi i\hbar)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{1}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{d}{2}-2}\mathrm{g}_{\ell}(\lambda)\bigg\{\int e^{i\lambda\tfrac{xHx}{2\hbar}}b_{\ell}(x,z)\mathrm{d}x\bigg\}\mathrm{d}\lambda

(A.7)

with
\mathrm{g}_{1}=1
and for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathrm{g}_{\ell+1}:[1,\infty)\to[0,1]
is the solution of

\mathrm{g}_{\ell+1}^{\prime}(\lambda)=\lambda^{-2}\mathrm{g}_{\ell}(\lambda)\,,\qquad\mathrm{g}_{\ell+1}(1)=0.

In particular,
\mathrm{g}_{\ell}
is non-decreasing and
\mathrm{g}_{\ell}(\infty)=\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\mathrm{g}_{\ell}(\lambda)
exists for every
\ell\geq 2
.
By an integration by parts, using (
A.5
)–(
A.6
),

\displaystyle R_{\ell}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{g}_{\ell+1}(\infty)i^{-\alpha}b_{\ell}(0,z)-(2\pi i\hbar)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{1}^{\infty}\mathrm{g}_{\ell+1}(\lambda)\partial_{\lambda}\bigg\{\lambda^{\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\lambda\tfrac{xHx}{2\hbar}}b_{\ell}(x,z)\mathrm{d}x\bigg\}\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\mathrm{g}_{\ell+1}(\infty)i^{-\alpha}b_{\ell}(0,z)+\hbar\delta^{-2}R_{\ell+1}(z).

By induction,
\displaystyle\mathrm{g}_{\ell+1}(\infty)=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{g}_{\ell}^{\prime}(\sigma)}{\sigma}\mathrm{d}\sigma=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{g}_{\ell-1}(\sigma)}{\sigma^{3}}\mathrm{d}\sigma=\cdots=\frac{1}{(\ell-1)!}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{g}_{1}(\sigma)}{\sigma^{\ell+1}}\mathrm{d}\sigma=\frac{1}{\ell!}
, so we conclude that for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

R_{0}(z)=i^{-\alpha}{\textstyle\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}}(\hbar\delta^{-2})^{k}b_{k}(0,z)/k!+(\hbar\delta^{-2})^{\ell+1}R_{\ell+1}(z).

Since
b_{k}=\delta^{2k}L^{k}b
for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, this proves (
A.3
) with
L^{k}=i^{-\alpha}\frac{(i\nabla\cdot H\nabla)^{k}}{k!}
in this coordinate system. Going back to the original coordinates,
L^{k}
are differential operators of degree
2k
whose coefficients depend only
on the phase
\Phi
with
L^{0}=i^{-\alpha}|\det\nabla_{x}^{2}\Phi|^{-1/2}
as a multiplication operator.
∎

Remark A.5
.

We give a slight generalization of Proposition
A.4
.
Let
\mathrm{g}:\Omega\times\mho\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, such that
\nabla\mathrm{g}\neq 0
on
\{|\mathrm{g}|<1\}
.
Let
a\in S^{\delta}(\Omega\times\mho)
with
\delta\geq\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
, assume that
\supp(a)\subset\{|\mathrm{g}|<1\}
and it holds for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon\in[\delta,1]
,

a=\mathcal{O}_{S^{\delta}}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\delta\epsilon}\big)^{\ell}\big)\qquad\text{uniformly on }\{|\mathrm{g}(x,z)|>\epsilon\}.

(A.8)

Then, setting
\mathrm{f}(z):=\mathrm{C}\mathrm{g}(x_{z},z)
for
z\in\mho
and some constant
\mathrm{C}\geq 1
, it holds for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon\in[\delta,1]
,

(2\pi i\hbar)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}_{S^{\delta}}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{\hbar}{\delta\epsilon}\bigg)^{\ell}\bigg)\qquad\text{uniformly on }\{|\mathrm{f}(z)|>\epsilon\}.

This follows directly by splitting the integral

(2\pi i\hbar)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\bigg\{\int e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\chi_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{g}(x,z))\mathrm{d}x+\int e^{i\tfrac{\Phi(x,z)}{\hbar}}a(x,z)\big\{1-\chi_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{g}(x,z))\big\}\mathrm{d}x\bigg\}

where
\chi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
is a smooth cutoff supported in
B_{1}
and equals to 1 on a neighborhood of
0
.
In particular, since
\epsilon\geq\delta
, both symbols are in
S^{\delta}
and the first integral satisfies (
A.4
) with
\mathcal{A}=\{|\mathrm{g}(x,z)|\leq\epsilon\}
. Moreover, since
\mathrm{g}
is not degenerate,

\big\{z\in\mho:|\mathrm{f}(z)|>\epsilon\big\}\subset\big\{z\in\mho:\dist\big((x_{z},z),\mathcal{A}\big)\geq\epsilon\big\}

if
\mathrm{C}
is sufficiently large.
By Lemma
A.2
, the second integral is also
\mathcal{O}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\delta\epsilon}\big)^{\infty}\big)
.

In the case of the “standard” phase
\Phi(x,\xi)=x\cdot\xi
, which is relevant for pseudo-differential calculus, we can also perform a stationary phase even in the case where the scalings in
x,\xi
are different and the amplitude does not necessarily belong to
S^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
with
\delta\geq\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Adapting the proof of Proposition
A.4
, we obtain the following statement.

Proposition A.6
.

Let
a:\mathbb{R}^{2n+m}\to\mathbb{R}
with
a\in S^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{x}\times S^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{\xi}\times S^{\eta}_{z}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\eta\in[\hbar,1]
and assume that
\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\geq\hbar
. Then for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, as
\hbar\to 0
,

\frac{1}{(2\pi i\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi,z)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi=\sum_{0\leq k<\ell}\frac{1}{k!}(i\hbar\partial_{x}\cdot\partial_{\xi})^{k}a(x,\xi,z)\big|_{x=\xi=0}+\mathcal{O}_{S^{\eta}}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}\big)^{\ell}\big).

(A.9)

Proof.

First, we record from the proof of Proposition
A.4
that if the phase is (independent of
z
) and given by

\Phi:X\mapsto\tfrac{X\cdot HX}{2}
where
H
is a constant non-degenerate matrix (
\det H\neq 0
), then the operator

L^{k}:=i^{-\alpha}(\det H)^{-1/2}\frac{(i\nabla\cdot H\nabla)^{k}}{k!},\qquad\text{for $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$}.

Moreover, in the special case
H=(\begin{smallmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
I_{n}&0\end{smallmatrix})
and
X=(x,\xi)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
(so that

\Phi(X)=x\cdot\xi
, if
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\geq\sqrt{\hbar}
, then the expansion (
A.9
) follows directly from Proposition
A.4
. Note that by differentiating under the integral, since
a\in S^{\eta}_{z}
, the error term is controlled in the class
S^{\eta}
.

We deal with the general case by a change of variable.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
\varepsilon_{1}\geq\varepsilon_{2}
and that the symbol
a
is independent of the parameter
z
. Let
(\gamma,\delta)=(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{2}/\varepsilon_{1}},\sqrt{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})
, let
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n},[0,1])
be such that
\chi=1
on
B_{n}
and, let
\theta^{k}(\xi)=\frac{1-\chi(\xi)}{|\xi|^{2k}}\xi^{\otimes k}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Note that
\gamma,\delta\in[\sqrt{\hbar},1]
and let
\chi_{\gamma}=\chi(\cdot/\gamma)
and
\theta^{k}_{\gamma}=\theta^{k}(\cdot/\gamma)
. We split

\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi=\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi)\chi_{\gamma}(\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi+\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi)\theta^{0}_{\gamma}(\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi.

We can perform repeated integration by parts in the second integral (writing
\tfrac{-i\hbar\xi\cdot\partial_{x}}{|\xi^{2}|}e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}=e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}
), we obtain for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi=\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi)\chi_{\gamma}(\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi+\frac{(i\hbar)^{k}}{(\varepsilon_{1}\gamma)^{k}}\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a_{k}(x,\xi)\cdot\theta^{k}_{\gamma}(\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi.

where
a_{k}=\varepsilon_{1}^{k}\partial_{x}^{k}a
is in
S^{\varepsilon_{1}}
and
\varepsilon_{1}\gamma=\delta
. Since
a_{k}
is uniformly bounded and compactly supported and
\big\|\theta^{k}_{\gamma}(\xi)\big\|_{L^{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{k}(1)
if
k>n
, this shows that

\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi=\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi)\chi_{\gamma}(\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty}).

Then, by rescaling (the phase and measure are invariant under this change of variables), letting

\widetilde{a}(x,\xi):=a(x/\gamma,\xi\gamma)\chi(\xi),

we have
\widetilde{a}\in S^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
(in particular this symbol has a fixed compact support and
\delta\geq\sqrt{\hbar}
) and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi i\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}\widetilde{a}(x,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{\infty})

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq k<\ell}\hbar^{k}L^{k}\widetilde{a}(x,\xi)\big|_{x=\xi=0}+\mathcal{O}\big((\hbar\delta^{-2})^{\ell}\big)

by applying Proposition
A.4
. Since

L^{k}\widetilde{a}=L^{k}a=\frac{1}{k!}(i\hbar\partial_{x}\cdot\partial_{\xi})^{k}a
in this case, this completes the proof.
∎

More specifically, we also need the following consequence of Proposition
A.6
.

Corollary A.7
.

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
a:\mathbb{R}^{2n+m}\to\mathbb{R}
, bounded with compact support, and assume that

\displaystyle(x,\xi)\mapsto\int a(x,\xi,z)\mathrm{d}z
is in
S^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{x}\times S^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{\xi}
for some
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in[\hbar,1]
with
\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\geq\hbar
.
Then for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, as
\hbar\to 0
,

\frac{1}{(2\pi i\hbar)^{n}}\int e^{i\tfrac{x\cdot\xi}{\hbar}}a(x,\xi,z)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}z=\sum_{0\leq k<\ell}\frac{1}{k!}\int(i\hbar\partial_{x}\cdot\partial_{\xi})^{k}a(x,\xi,z)\big|_{x=\xi=0}\mathrm{d}z+\mathcal{O}\Big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}\big)^{\ell}\Big).

Proof.

By assumption, we can apply Proposition
A.6
to the symbol

\displaystyle(x,\xi)\mapsto\int a(x,\xi,z)\mathrm{d}z.

This yields the required expansion. Note that since
a
is smooth with a fixed compact support, one can differentiate under the integral with respect to
z
.
∎

Corollary A.8
.

Let
m,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\delta\in[\hbar,1]
with
\varepsilon_{1}\leq\delta
and
\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\geq\hbar
.
Let
(t,\sigma,z)\mapsto a(t,\sigma,z)
be in
S^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{t}(\mathbb{R})\times S^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R})
and supported in
\{z\in\mho\}
with
\mho\Subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
open
(\mho
is allowed to depend on the scales
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\delta)
.
Assume that
a(t,\sigma,z)=\mathcal{O}_{S^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{t}\times S^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{\sigma}}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\varepsilon\delta}\big)^{\ell}\big)
uniformly for
t\in[-\delta,\delta],\sigma\in\mathbb{R},z\in\mho
, then as
\hbar\to 0
,

\frac{1}{2\pi i\hbar}\int e^{i\tfrac{t\sigma}{\hbar}}a(t,\sigma,z)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}z=\mathcal{O}\Big(|\mho|\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\varepsilon\delta}\big)^{\ell}\Big),\qquad\varepsilon=\min(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}).

Let
\mathrm{g}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, such that
\{|\mathrm{g}|\leq 1\}
,
\partial_{x}\mathrm{g}\neq 0
on
\{\mathrm{g}=0\}
is compact, and
a\in S^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{t}(\mathbb{R})\times S^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R})
is supported in
\{|\mathrm{g}|\leq 1\}
.
In particular, if it holds for
\ell\geq 2
and
\delta\in[\varepsilon_{1},1]

a(t,\sigma,z)=\mathcal{O}_{S^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{t}\times S^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{\sigma}}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\varepsilon\delta}\big)^{\ell}\big)\text{ uniformly for $t\in[-\delta,\delta],\sigma\in\mathbb{R},z\in\{|\mathrm{g}|\geq\delta\}$}

then

\frac{1}{2\pi i\hbar}\int e^{i\tfrac{t\sigma}{\hbar}}a(t,\sigma,z)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}z=\mathcal{O}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\varepsilon\varepsilon_{1}}\big)^{\ell}\big).

Proof.

We simply introduce a smooth cutoff
\chi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
such that
\chi=0
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
and
\chi^{\dagger}=1-\chi
satisfies
\chi^{\dagger}=0
on
[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
. Then, by repeated integrations by part, it holds for
z\in\mho
,

\int e^{i\tfrac{t\sigma}{\hbar}}a(t,\sigma,z)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\sigma=\int e^{i\tfrac{t\sigma}{\hbar}}\chi(t/\delta)a(t,\sigma,z)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\sigma+\big(\tfrac{i\hbar}{\eta\delta}\big)^{k}\int e^{i\tfrac{t\sigma}{\hbar}}\tfrac{\chi^{\dagger}(t/\delta)}{(t/\delta)^{k}}\eta^{k}\partial_{\sigma}^{k}a(t,\sigma,z)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\sigma

By assumption, both symbols
(t,\sigma)\mapsto\chi(t/\delta)a(t,\sigma,z)
and

(t,\sigma)\mapsto\tfrac{\chi^{\dagger}(t/\delta)}{(t/\delta)^{k}}\eta^{k}\partial_{\sigma}^{k}a(t,\sigma,z)

are

\mathcal{O}_{S^{\varepsilon_{1}}_{t}\times S^{\varepsilon_{2}}_{\sigma}}\big(\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\varepsilon\delta}\big)^{k}\big)
uniformly for
z\in\mho
, hence Proposition
A.6
shows that the first integral is
\mathcal{O}\big(\hbar\big(\tfrac{\hbar}{\varepsilon\delta}\big)^{k}\big)
and the second is
\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
uniformly for
z\in\mho
.
Then, by integrating over
z\in\mho
, this completes the proof.
∎
